**The Primordial Ooze**

  

Baseball wasn’t so much invented as cobbled together. There was a big group of bat-ball-base games - rounders, cricket, town ball, old cat - that came together differently in different regions of America (and England, for that matter). In Massachusetts baseball, pitchers threw overhand, and in Philadelphia town ball, there were no foul balls. (In both, home plate was in between two bases and you could get a runner out by drilling them with the ball.) The New York version eventually won out.

  

We begin baseball history with the formation of the Knickerbocker Base Ball Club in September 1845, not because they invented baseball, but because they were influential in the early days and, more importantly, because their activities were documented.

  

The Knickerbocker Club emerged as a more focused, tightly-constructed unit out of the more loosely-organized New York Ball Club. Alexander Cartwright, who is in the Hall of Fame today, suggested to a few playing mates that they form their own club, and they were off and running. Insurance broker Duncan Curry was the club’s first president, after which Doc Adams, a 30-year-old doctor, Yale undergrad and Harvard medical graduate, was president for a decade or so. (Cartwright wasn’t particularly involved after the formation of the group, and had moved to the west coast within a couple of years.)

  

They were a group of young, well-off, well-educated types in New York City, which meant that they mostly had fairly sedentary jobs. Playing baseball, which in any of its many forms was a kids’ game, was their way of getting in some exercise after work.

  

William Wheaton and William Tucker, both original Knickerbockers, put together the oldest extant list of rules shortly after, a list of 20 that included things like “be on time” in addition to the distance between the bases. Wheaton was a 31-year-old lawyer and Tucker was a tobacconist in his mid-20s. Essentially, this is when somebody (a) got serious enough about the game to stop arguing about exactly which game they were going to be playing when a group got together to play baseball, (b) wrote it all down, and (c) had the longevity and influence to make those rules standard.

  

I’ll note here, not the rules that they had, but the differences from what we’d consider to be normal for baseball today:

  - No set number of players.
  - Pitching was underhand, with the goal being to start the action, rather than get the ball past the hitter. Balls and called strikes were yet to be created.
  - The ball was super light, and Doc Adams handmade all of them for a few years.
  - The bases were probably in the range of about 75 feet until the Civil War.
  - For awhile, the winner was the first to 21 runs, as opposed to worrying about the number of innings.
  - Hit balls caught on one hop were outs.

  

They mostly just played among themselves, but the first intersquad game we have on record was the Knickerbocker Club getting blasted 23-1 by their old friends at the New York Ball Club in four innings. Hard to give the Knickerbockers *full* credit for inventing the sport when they immediately had a much better opponent to lose to. These were just the dweebs who were excited about rules and keeping notes, rather than the most elite athletes playing the game at the time. The game was played at the Knickerbockers’ main grounds, Elysian Fields in Hoboken, NJ, just across the river from Manhattan, on what was essentially a big empty meadow.

  

By 1857, there were enough clubs cropping up in New York that it was worth creating an overriding organization to keep everyone on the same page. Adams was elected president and held it until he retired from medicine and moved to Connecticut in the early 1860s. This is when game length was set to nine innings. Their meeting in 1858, when they named themselves the National Association of Base Ball Players, was when the bases were set at 90 feet apart, the pitching distance was standardized at 45 feet, and called strikes were introduced. This is also the time frame when the press, particularly Henry Chadwick, started paying real attention to the game, and the versions of baseball being played in Philadelphia and Massachusetts got swallowed up by the New York game.

  

Money started creeping into the game with press coverage and gambling interest. Sometimes teams started charging for admission around this time. There was about a decade, from the late 1850s through the late 1860s, when all the top clubs were nominally amateur, but there were certainly a few guys getting paid under the table. One of these was baseball’s first real star, Jim Creighton.

  

The center of the baseball universe had shifted the few miles from New York to Brooklyn by 1860, and that was the year that 19-year-old Jim Creighton became a sensation with Brooklyn’s Excelsior club. He was the hardest-throwing pitcher, and one of the better hitters, the game had yet seen, and there was constant debate over whether his pitching delivery was legal. He was the best player on likely baseball’s best team, as Excelsior was dominant on the sport’s first major barnstorming tour in 1860, traveling throughout the east coast from Washington to Canada.

  

Two years later, with the Civil War pulling many players away from the field, Creighton and Excelsior were still atop the baseball world. In an October game against Union of Morrisania, Creighton was 4-for-4 with four doubles when he homered in his fifth at-bat. After crossing the plate he removed himself from the game with an abdominal injury, supposedly from swinging the bat too hard. He died four days later of a ruptured hernia at age 21. It seems like the exact circumstances of Creighton’s death are up for debate, but it’s viable enough that it’s worth perpetuating the myth.

  

The war stunted baseball’s growth for a few years, with one strange but important consequence: Because attendance at the annual NABBP meeting was so thin, the consistently-rejected “fly game” rule (meaning that only fly balls caught in the air were outs, no longer including one-hop catches) finally passed.

  

Another oddity of this period: In the early 1860s, ice baseball became briefly popular, with an extra fielder behind the catcher and a rule that you could overrun the bases. (This is the origin of the rule that a runner is allowed to overrun first base.)

  

At the beginning of the decade, Brooklyn (with the Excelsior, Eckford, and Atlantic clubs) and New York (with Mutual and Union) pretty consistently fielded the top teams. By the middle of the decade, the Athletic club of Philadelphia was excellent as well.

  

In 1866, the Pythian club of Philadelphia was founded by 27-year-old teacher and civil rights activist Octavius Catto, and they quickly became the first strong Black team in America. After the 1867 season, they campaigned to become a part of the NABBP and were rejected. They were impressive enough in Philadelphia that, by late 1869, the better white teams in town felt enough public pressure to play against them. These Pythians games were the first recorded high-level integrated baseball games, and, after a couple of shots at it, the first recorded Black team beating a white team.

  

At this point, a typical score is like 40-10. Attendance isn’t super consistent, but there are sometimes 1000+ fans at games in the late 1850s and reports of 30,000 fans at a game in Philadelphia shortly after the Civil War. Gambling is a major reason attendance starts to swell in the early days, and, honestly, a major reason guys start getting paid. It’s tough to keep the sport running when the league refuses to allow players to be paid but gamblers are happy to pay them to throw games.

**1869 season**

**1870 season**

  

Around this time, the baseball world started to revolve around Harry Wright. Harry was born in 1835 in Sheffield, England, and came to America as a toddler. His father ran the St. George’s Cricket Club in New York, and Harry worked for a few years at Tiffany’s jewelry while playing cricket and baseball around the city. He put in time with the Knickerbockers, starting in the late 1850s, and later Excelsior and Gotham, but things started to change for him when he moved to Cincinnati in 1865 to take a job as a club cricket pro. He took over the reins of Cincinnati Base Ball Club when it formed the next year and started recruiting help. By 1867 they were good, going 17-1 with their single loss coming to a Washington barnstorming group led by Harry’s younger brother, George, and pitcher Asa Brainard, who had previously replaced Jim Creighton as the top pitcher for Excelsior. That one loss was by the score of 53-10, and it inspired Cincinnati ownership to start recruiting nationally and paying players. Brainard was lured to Cincinnati for the 1868 season, likely because Harry was open to professionalism before the NABBP was.

  

For the 1869 season, Cincinnati decided to pay all nine players, the first team to do so (or, at the very least, the first to do so openly). Harry added Cal McVey, who’d be one of the better players in baseball for much of the 1870s, from an Indianapolis team that Cincinnati had smoked in 1868. And the most important signing was George Wright, who was probably the best player in the world, out of New York, to play shortstop. There are differing sources but their team salary was definitely a record for the time, a little over $9000 (about $180,000 today). The NABBP, shortly after this, decided to allow professional teams.

  

Cincinnati scarcely were able to fund an East Coast trip that resulted in wins over all the top New York teams of the day. By the time they made it back home, money was less of an issue, and the months-old transcontinental railroad made a western trip viable for the first time. (Remember, this is all well before cars, so travel meant a train and, if necessary, a stagecoach or a steam car from the train station to the field.)

  
  

Cincy blasted San Francisco.

Finished 57-0, some argued Atlantics were the champs, under a “heavyweight belt” type of championship understanding. 

Chicago loaded up for 1870, spending $18,000 on players.

Called balls 1863. 

And as late as the 1850s, it wasn’t entirely clear whether the New York or Massachusetts version of baseball was going to win out. The main differences in the Massachusetts game:

1\. The hitter stood halfway between home and first, rather than at home.

2\. You could get a runner out by pegging them with the ball.

3\. Pitchers threw overhand instead of underhand.

4\. One out per side.

  

Not least of all because some of the Brooklyn teams who toured in the early 1860s smoked the Boston clubs, the New York game won out by the end of the Civil War.

1860: Asa Brainard Excelsior, Jimmy Wood Eckford

1867: Cherokee Fisher Philadelphia, Davy Force Washington

1868: Ross Barnes Rockford, Deacon White Cleveland, Cal McVey Indianapolis, Dave Eggler New York

  

*Sources: Baseball in the Garden of Eden by John Thorn, Strike Four by Richard Hershberger,* ***Baseball Revolutionaries by Greg Rhodes***

**The most successful team in baseball history to that point was the Cincinnati Red Stockings of 1869 and 1870, managed by Harry Wright. Despite two dominant seasons (including a 67-0 record in 1869, put together while barnstorming), the team’s financial backers decided it was getting too expensive and shut the team down. Boston resident Ivers Adams, who earned his money as the president of the American Net and Teone Company, had put together $15,000 (about $300,000 today) to start a Boston professional baseball team. Adams tracked down Wright, along with his brother, George, debatably the best baseball player in America at that point, and convinced them to take the reins of his Boston squad. The Wright brothers also convinced Cincinnati teammate Cal McVey to come along and catch for them. And perhaps most importantly, they raided the two best young players from Rockford Forest City, 20-year-old pitcher Al Spalding and 21-year-old second baseman Ross Barnes, who would be the best pitcher and hitter in baseball for the next five years.**

  

**Chi**

  

**Boston fell just short of winning the 1871 league title, not least of all because they lost George Wright for half the short season to injury. With their shortstop around all season, they won the 1872 title in a walk. McVey signed on to manage Baltimore the following season, but they convinced 25-year-old catcher Deacon White, formerly of Cleveland, to unretire, and found themselves, if anything, better than before. For 1874, they signed McVey back and stuck him in the outfield on their way to their third straight league title, then capped it off with a dominant 71-8 record in 1875, when Barnes, George Wright, Spalding, White, and McVey were probably the five best players in baseball.**

  

**Midway through the 1875 season, though, Chicago owner William Hulbert decided something needed to be done about Boston’s dominance and quietly signed away Barnes, Spalding, McVey, and White for the 1876 campaign. Sensing that this probably wouldn’t sit well, Hulbert decided to start the National League, beginning with his newly-strong Chicago franchise. And while Chicago did take the 1876 league championship, George Wright led the Boston squad, re-dubbed the Red Caps to avoid confusion with the NL’s Cincinnati Red Stockings, to a respectable fourth-place finish.**

  

**That was Wright’s last really good year, but Boston re-signed White from Chicago, who had an MVP-level year for the Red Caps at first base. They also signed pitcher Tommy Bond, who was quickly replacing Spalding as the best pitcher in the league, away from Hartford, after a months-long despite stemming from Bond accusing a teammate of throwing a game after a loss. (There’s no evidence, but... it’s not like no games were being thrown in the 1870s.) These additions, along with Chicago’s troubles - losing White, Barnes’s career falling apart after a long and mysterious illness, and Spalding’s retirement at age 26 to take over the world with his sporting goods company - put Boston in position to win another league title in 1877. And even after White left for Cincinnati after the season, Bond almost singlehandedly led the Red Caps to their sixth championship in eight years in 1878.**

  

**After both of these NL titles, Boston took on the winners of other leagues, and struggled more than one might expect. For one, they lost a 2-1 series to the 1877 New England Association champion - the truly ridiculously named Lowell (MA) Ladies’ Men. (Trust me, if there were more readily-available information about the Ladies’ Men, I would relay it here.) They also took losses in two other postseason series in 1877, and split one in 1878 - they were probably the best team in the world those two seasons, but we’re definitely not at the point yet where MLB is truly the only game in town.**

  

**Wright tried to refuel the anemic offense by signing outfielder Charley Jones away from Cincinnati, and he had the best season of his career in 1879, and Bond was excellent again, but, despite being a bit better than the year before, they couldn’t catch Providence and finished second.**

  

**After two seasons below .500, and Bond throwing his arm out late in 1880, Wright took the Providence Grays’ managerial post before the 1882 season. John Morrill, the Caps’ 25-year-old first baseman, took over for the rest of the decade.**

**​**

**Cover Charley Jones ban in 1880**

  

**Boston continued, under Morrill, to replace departing stars with new ones, and by 1882 they’d signed the two pitchers who would lead them to yet another NL crown in 1883: Jim Whitney, who was also one of the better hitters in the majors in the early 1880s, and Charlie Buffinton. By this point, their most commonly-used nickname is the Beaneaters.**

  

**​Despite Boston’s continued success and stability, there hadn’t yet been a significant attempt to add a second team to the city, as there had been in New York, Philadelphia, and Washington. That changes in 1884, when former Boston star George Wright puts together a team to play in the fledgling Union Association, called the Boston Reds. But the Reds are a middling team in a poorly-organized league and fold along with the UA after just one season.****

**1871**

New leagues: National Association

A lot of the writing here could come from Major League Baseball article on Weebly

three balls for a walk but don’t have to call every pitch a ball or a strike, no ball/strike on first pitch, strike zone first defined (knee to shoulder) and split into high/low zones the batter could request, confusion about if you had to retouch first after running through before going to second

As I see it, major league baseball (and American professional team sports) was founded with the formation of the National Association of Professional Base Ball Players in 1871. There had been professional teams previously, and for the previous couple of years with some degree of regularity, but this is where they started focusing on playing against each other, rather than barnstorming through America and playing whatever local team happened to be nearby.

  

The National Association rose from the ashes of the Cincinnati Red Stockings, a barnstorming professional team led by centerfielder / manager / general manager / promoter Harry Wright. They famously went undefeated in 1869, and, after repeating as unofficial national champions in 1870, disbanded due to expense.

  

The original nine included the Boston Red Stockings, the only franchise that has been running continuously since. They aren't as familiar as they appear, though, as they wound up sticking in the National League and eventually becoming the Atlanta Braves. The Philadelphia Athletics and Chicago White Stockings are also recognizable names, but neither makes it all the way through the 1870s, and the White Stockings aren't as familiar as they appear, either, eventually becoming the Cubs.

  

To round things out, we've got teams in Washington and New York, we've got two teams named Forest City (one in Cleveland, the other in Rockford, IL), as well as the Fort Wayne Kekiongas and the Troy Haymakers. The first game in baseball's major league history was not Red Sox-Yankees, but Kekiongas-Cleveland Forest City in Fort Wayne, Indiana. Here's a team picture of Forest City:

  
  

​Troy isn't as odd a major league town as it appears to us today - it was the 28th biggest city in America in 1870, with around 50,000 people. (It has essentially the same population now, although that leaves it the 13th biggest city in the state of New York.) Fort Wayne and Rockford, on the other hand, are below 20,000, small cities even then. I suspect the reason Rockford is considered viable is that, the previous season, they employed both 2B Ross Barnes and SP Al Spalding, who are probably the best players in baseball from 1871-75, but who also get signed away from them before the season starts.

  

​We don't go south of DC or west of Rockford. It's odd that there's no team in Brooklyn, still separate from New York City and the nation's third-largest city, as there were strong Brooklyn clubs as far back as the 1830s and 1840s. St. Louis, the nation's fourth-largest city, is oddly missing at this point. But the dearth of teams in the south and west has as much do with the lack of viable major league-type cities in those areas as it does with the fact that the game started in the northeast. Other than San Francisco and New Orleans, there really aren't any of the 30 or so biggest cities of the time period that would stretch the map past DC or St. Louis, which is part of the reason we're 80 years away from really doing that.

The season lasts around 30 games, and Philadelphia wins the league with a 22-7 record, led by third baseman Levi Meyerle, who hits .492. They essentially play ten players, total, all season long. There's a game in June where Philly beats Troy 49-33. Most guys in the league are in their early 20s, because "professional baseball player" is still a pretty stupid career plan. A 19-year-old Bobby Mathews, throws every inning of the season for Fort Wayne, and they don't even finish last. The top earning player in the league is making in the neighborhood of $1500 for the season, the equivalent of about $30,000 today. The rules, until the following spring, banned the use of the wrist in pitching - and that's not to mention overhand pitching, which wasn't allowed until 1884. Nine balls for a walk. Pitchers are throwing off flat ground, 45 feet away. Nobody's wearing gloves. Over the course of a game, the ball gets beaten up and bounces worse. No groundskeepers. Boston's third baseman, Harry Schafer, commits 59 errors in 31 games.

  

Fort Wayne falls apart midway through the 1871 season, and is unofficially replaced by the Brooklyn Eckfords immediately.

  
Great Chicago Fire happens right before Chicago faces off with Philly for the title.

**1872**

overrunning/retouching first rule clarified, captains could no longer agree to call a game a draw

Rockford also doesn't make it to 1872 after a last place finish, nor does Chicago, who finished second, after their stadium burned down in the Great Chicago Fire of October 1871. They're replaced by the Eckfords, a second Brooklyn team, a second Washington team, the successful Baltimore Canaries, and, bizarrely, the Middletown Mansfields of Middletown, CT. So to start the season we're at 11 teams, and all but Cleveland sit in a little band from Boston to DC:

​\<iframe title="Map: 1872 National Association" aria-describedby="" id="datawrapper-chart-ZSEKP" src="//datawrapper.dwcdn.net/ZSEKP/1/" scrolling="no" frameborder="0" style="width: 0; min-width: 100% \!important;" height="400"\>\</iframe\>\<script type="text/javascript"\>\!function(){"use strict";window.addEventListener("message",function(a){if(void 0\!==a.data\["datawrapper-height"\])for(var t in a.data\["datawrapper-height"\]){var e=document.getElementById("datawrapper-chart-"+t);e&&(e.style.height=a.data\["datawrapper-height"\]\[t\]+"px")}})}();\</script\>  
Middletown, both Washington teams, Troy, and Cleveland disband before the season ends, and the Eckfords have to take on a big chunk of the Troy roster to finish the season 3-26. Boston gets out to a 22-1 start and wins the league, as they will in 1873, 1874, and 1875, and as they nearly did the previous year. There's a real argument that in 1875 the five best players in the world all play for Boston: Barnes, Spalding, SS George Wright, C Deacon White, 1B Cal McVey. Players are jumping from team to team wildly - Candy Cummings, one of the better pitchers of the early 1870s and the purported inventor of the curveball, pitched for a different team every year from 1871-75.

  

**1873**

throw/jerk in pitching motion legalized (break wrist), both captains had to agree to allow a courtesy runner  
The Eckfords also drop out, though the Brooklyn Atlantics make it through the season while pitching 16-year-old Jim Britt every inning. (Britt pitches essentially every inning again for the Atlantics in 1873 at age 17, to below average results, then is never recorded playing pro baseball again.) They and the remaining five teams (Boston, Baltimore, Philadelphia Athletics, New York) are joined by four more new teams for the NA's third season in 1873. A second Baltimore team loses their first five games and disbands, and the Resolutes of Elizabeth, NJ go 2-21 before following suit.

  

It's easy to say 150 years later, but it is strange how the NA failed to learn from its mistakes with putting teams up in these smaller towns. Your six consistently successful, money-making franchises (the five holdovers, plus Chicago) are six of the seven biggest cities in the U.S. (excepting St. Louis), each with over a quarter million people. Troy, Fort Wayne, Rockford, and Middletown all struggle, all with less than 50,000 people, yet the league throws a team into Elizabeth regardless.

  

The other two new teams are a replacement in Washington, the Blue Legs, who appear to fold before the season's out, and a successful second team in Philadelphia, the White Stockings. The 1873 season is even more tightly collected in that northeastern corridor:

Games in this time period are typically attended by a couple thousand people and are heavily bet on. And we're still missing some shockingly basic-seeming things in baseball. There's a game in July where Boston leads Elizabeth 11-3 entering the bottom of the ninth, but they don't stop the game - and Boston puts 21 more runs on the hapless Resolutes to win 32-3. There's another game where 40 errors are committed. In a third game, Bob Ferguson of the Brooklyn Atlantics is umpiring a game between New York and Baltimore, and, during a postgame altercation with New York's Nat Hicks, breaks his arm with a bat. To join the league, all your team needs is ten bucks ($228 today) for the NA entry fee. It all makes the deadball 1910s feel stunningly modern by comparison.

  

The Baltimore Marylands have what is probably the worst team in NA history, losing all six of their games by scores of 24-3, 27-7, 26-5, 20-0, 35-1, 20-10 before falling off the map.

  

**1874**

walk on three wides or nine balls, now every pitch is called (including first pitch), wrong zone was a ball and out of the zone was a wide, batter’s box replaces batter’s line, HBP made a dead ball (previously hitters would get hit on purpose to aid base stealers), in an infield fly rule attempt tried to define “momentarily held balls” as caught to avoid purposeful drops (didn’t fix the rule entirely until 1894)  
We start 1874 with fully six returning franchises, and a seventh (Chicago) back after a short and understandable break. The eighth NA team for the season is the Hartford Dark Blues, and everybody finishes the year out for the first time. Baltimore's four best players get swiped and they go 9-38 in a full season, even though Boston plays 70 games. More strange than the games played disparity is the fact that Boston and the Philadelphia Athletics play zero league games from July 15 to September 10, while they went on an exhibition tour of England.

  

**1875**

balls & wides combined - still nine for a walk  
Boston wins the 1875 championship by a wide margin, finishing 71-8. Baltimore doesn't make it to 1875, but everybody else does. There's a clear split in the standings this season: Seven teams (Boston, both holdover Philly teams, Hartford, Chicago, New York, and the new St. Louis Brown Stockings) all play 65+ games and are basically .500 or better teams. Brooklyn is the club this year who loses their four best players and falls to pieces, though they play essentially a full season at 2-42. And the new team in New Haven, Elm City, at least finishes the season out, though they go 7-40. The other four new teams disband much earlier - a second St. Louis team, a third Philadelphia team, a fifth attempt to get a team in Washington, and the short-lived Keokuk (IA) Westerns.  
\<iframe title="Map: 1875 National Association" aria-describedby="" id="datawrapper-chart-o7NkZ" src="//datawrapper.dwcdn.net/o7NkZ/1/" scrolling="no" frameborder="0" style="width: 0; min-width: 100% \!important;" height="400"\>\</iframe\>\<script type="text/javascript"\>\!function(){"use strict";window.addEventListener("message",function(a){if(void 0\!==a.data\["datawrapper-height"\])for(var t in a.data\["datawrapper-height"\]){var e=document.getElementById("datawrapper-chart-"+t);e&&(e.style.height=a.data\["datawrapper-height"\]\[t\]+"px")}})}();\</script\>

  

**1876**

New leagues: National League

tag ups allowed on foul outs, made it so hitters got a warning on a called third strike before being called out on the next one, Spalding ball named official ball of the National League which ended an era of lively/dead ball arguments (ball cost about $1 or $30 today - players would cut/throw away balls before their team came up to bat so they’d get a new one), fair-foul bunt outlawed before 1876 postseason exhibitions (and stuck for 1877)  
Chicago owner William Hulbert decides to do something about Boston's dominance, and signs Barnes, Spalding, White, and McVey - the four best players in the league who aren't related to Boston manager Harry Wright - to 1876 contracts midway through the 1875 season. Rather than face getting his new stars suspended, since he'd signed them away from their teams so early, he decided to start the National League. Five of the other six stable teams from the NA follow him, excepting the Philadelphia White Stockings, but including the wronged Boston squad. We also add the Louisville Grays, in the nation's 14th-biggest market, and the Cincinnati Reds, named after the 1869-70 traveling club whose breakup led to the creation of the NA.  
\<iframe title="1876 National League" aria-label="USA states Symbol map" id="datawrapper-chart-WXygm" src="//datawrapper.dwcdn.net/WXygm/1/" scrolling="no" frameborder="0" style="width: 0; min-width: 100% \!important;" height="400"\>\</iframe\>\<script type="text/javascript"\>\!function(){"use strict";window.addEventListener("message",function(a){if(void 0\!==a.data\["datawrapper-height"\])for(var t in a.data\["datawrapper-height"\]){var e=document.getElementById("datawrapper-chart-"+t);e&&(e.style.height=a.data\["datawrapper-height"\]\[t\]+"px")}})}();\</script\>  
Chicago probably the most dominant team in major league history.  
  

  

**1877**

New leagues: International Association (Pud Galvin, Pittsburgh)  
first catcher’s mask developed at Harvard(?), commonplace by the end of 1878, often would only wear mask with runners on so they could get close to the hitter (and would stand back for foul bound opportunities with bases empty)  
After also signing Cap Anson, who’ll be one of the league’s better players for the next 15 years, Chicago marches to the league title. Philadelphia and New York fail to complete their seasons, and Hulbert kicks them out of the league.

  

The six leftover teams are the only ones in the National League in 1877, though the Hartford team moves to Brooklyn - away from their most famous fan, Mark Twain - and starts calling themselves the Brooklyn Hartfords. Ross Barnes and Al Spalding, and Chicago along with them, both see their careers fall off a cliff. Barnes gets a mysterious illness that ruins his season, and essentially causes him to be finished as a player at age 27. Spalding, after being the best pitcher in the world a year previously, stopped pitching in order to become a full-time first baseman - it's unclear how much of this decision was inspired by arm trouble, though it's certainly odd that he abandoned pitching during the worst hitting season of his career. On the other hand, he voluntarily retired after playing one game in 1878 (also at age 27) to start the Spalding sporting goods company that remains a monolith today. Deacon White, the resultant best player in baseball after Barnes's steep decline, jumps from Chicago to Boston, and along with new pitcher Tommy Bond, lead Boston to the 1877 title.

  

Even including the five years of the NA, 1877 & 1878 are really the only years from 1871 to today where the major league champion (Boston, in both cases) is only debatably the best baseball team in the world. Over the course of the season, the six NL teams only go 24-22 against teams in the New England Association, and the NEA champion Lowell Ladies' Men finish \#2 in a U.S. newspaper poll of the best teams in America. Boston loses short postseason battles with Lowell and the New York State champion Syracuse Stars in 1877, and split with the champion Buffalo Bisons of the International Association in 1878.

walk on six balls, NL started ordering teams by W% instead of W, overhand legalized in NL, NL required both feet on the ground for entire delivery for two months before changing the rule back, AA gave first base on HBP if umpire judged it unavoidable, start seeing left glove more padded than right for catchers, inflatable chest protector popularized, shinguards were attempted but didn’t stick til 1900s

foul bound rule abolished in AA, overhand legalized in AA, (188?) AA put a slab made out of marble or glass at the front of the pitchers’ box to prevent pitchers from going past the front, fielding gloves pretty common at this point

Frank Grant - Meriden of Eastern League / Buffalo of International Association

walk on five balls, strikeout on four strikes, high & low strike zones combined, made it so back foot had to start on the back line of the pitcher’s box, pitching distance changed to 55’6 (from 45 foot line at the front of the pitcher’s box), NL gave first base on HBP,   
WS Game 6 wild

**\#1 Pedro Martinez**

**Here's a quick interview where Pedro talks about bringing Ortiz to Boston. Great clips of him acting like a goofball with the World Series trophy from 0:30-0:45.**

  

[**https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=2O4yvBVIUgM**](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=2O4yvBVIUgM)

**PHYSICAL DESCRIPTION: Pedro was a little guy, particularly for a more recent pitcher, at 5'11" and 170 pounds, closer to 150 as a young guy. He was just swimming in his uniform early on.**

**FASTBALL: Very good, never the fastest in the league. Threw in the mid-90s in his youth, at his peak he was in the low 90s, high 80s when he left Boston. Man, did it move, though, lots of rise on his four-seamer and tail on his two-seamer. Also threw a cutter at times.**

**CONTROL: Good, got into very good territory at his peak.**

**OFF-SPEED: Impossible. His changeup had ridiculous movement and was 10+ mph slower than his fastball, regularly described as one of the greatest of all-time. Had a nasty curveball and a slider he could play off it. Here's a great highlight reel from a 1999 game, which includes an entirely unhittable changeup at the 0:48 mark:**

[**https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=aQyLQFIhS4U**](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=aQyLQFIhS4U)

**DURABILITY: Pretty good. Mostly stayed healthy throughout his 20s, but wasn't a guy who was leading the league in innings even when he was. Tore his rotator cuff in 2001 to cut his outrageously high peak short, but pitched very effectively until 2006, when another rotator cuff tear all but ended his career at age 34.**

  

**Pedro grew up poor in Monoguayabo, Dominican Republic, a few miles outside Santo Domingo. His dad, who had some success as an amateur pitcher in the DR in the 1950s, was a janitor, and his mom was a laundress. The Martínez boys grew up playing baseball, with oranges and tree branches if need be. By the time Pedro was a teenager, his brother, Ramón, who was four years older, was a serious pitching prospect. Ramón was on the 1984 Dominican Olympic team at age 16 and had a minor league contract with the Dodgers by the end of the year.**

  

**​Ramón started in the Dodgers' Dominican academy, and, since it was only about an hour away from home, 13-year-old Pedro would tag along and throw the ball around, as well. By 1988, Ramón was one of the best pitching prospects in baseball, a lanky, 6'4" flamethrower, and pitched a little in the majors for Los Angeles. The Dodgers signed Pedro, now 16, to their Dominican academy, as well.**

  

**After a 1991 season that included a dominant stint in Double-A San Antonio, the 19-year-old Pedro was named the \#10 prospect in baseball by Baseball America. By now, Ramón was one of the better young pitchers in baseball.**

  

**Pedro got called up the last week of the 1992 season and threw eight solid innings. He was so flabbergasted when the Dodgers sent him back to Albuquerque to start the 1993 season that he threatened to go back to the Dominican. (He was back in LA to stay by April 11.) He spent the season as the Dodgers \#2 reliever and finished ninth in the Rookie of the Year voting, losing to his catcher, Mike Piazza.**

  

**When below average Dodger second baseman Jody Reed came to the negotiating table with a request for a big contract after the season, Los Angeles sent Pedro to Montreal for young second baseman Delino DeShields. (Though not a terrible bet by the Dodgers, DeShields would go on to play three mediocre seasons in LA before leaving in free agency.) Pedro called Ramón in tears with the news.****  
****  
****He put together a really nice season in his first as a starter. Montreal played in one playoff series in their history, but they appeared to be well on their way to a second when the 1994 strike started. He had two more solid seasons in 1995 and 1996, though the Expos weren’t quite the same playoff threat after the departure of Larry Walker. Pedro, during this period, developed a reputation as a headhunter, which was not entirely unearned - his HBP per walk and HBP per wild pitch ratios are pretty darn high. But that was basically his claim to fame until 1997.**

  

**Pedro put together an electric season in 1997, two years out from free agency. He leapfrogged into the best-pitcher-in-baseball discussion. But by this point, Pedro was essentially the last major piece of the strong 1994 team remaining, and the Expos finished below .500. Pedro was pretty expensive for the Expos already, making $3.6 million in 1997, but after his great season, he was entering his final season of arbitration (which eventually meant he made $7.6 million in 1998, more than triple any Expo) and would hit free agency the next fall. Briefly, it seemed like he might get sent to the Yankees for Mariano Rivera, but that stalled out, and Colorado and Boston were the finalists. Dan Duquette, the general manager who had brought Pedro to Montreal, was now running baseball operations in Boston, and he worked out a trade to bring Pedro to Boston for top pitching prospect Carl Pavano and Tony Armas, Jr. Pedro was originally mad about the trade, but within a few weeks signed a 6-year, $75 million extension to make him the highest-paid player in baseball.** 

  

**He had a really strong season in 1998 and helped propel the Sox into the playoffs. He had one start, in the ALDS against Cleveland, where he led Boston to their first playoff win since 1986. They lost three close ones after that, though, and the Sox exited meekly.**

  

**The Pedro of 1999 and 2000 is where one might get the idea that he’s the greatest ever. Over that pair of seasons, he was above 12 strikeouts per 9 innings and below two walks. He had a 1.90 ERA in a hitter’s park at the peak of major league offense. Pedro essentially had consecutive Sandy Koufax seasons, but in the hardest possible conditions instead of the easiest.**

  

**In a 1999 divisional series rematch with Cleveland, Pedro threw four shutout innings in Game 1 before straining a muscle in his back. Boston fought to force a deciding Game 5 without him, and it wasn’t entirely clear, at least to the public, whether Pedro would be available. But after two Red Sox pitchers got crushed early, Pedro entered in the bottom of the 4th into a game tied 8-8. He was throwing almost sidearm, with five MPH gone off his fastball, but he threw six shutout innings and the Sox took the series before losing to the Yankees in five games in the ALCS. (Pedro started Game 3 and threw seven shutout innings.)**

  

**The next year, Boston rolled out the best pitcher ever, Nomar Garciaparra, and not much else, and failed to make the playoffs. They added Manny Ramirez for the 2001 season, but Pedro missed two months with a rotator cuff issue and Nomar was out almost the entire season, so they missed the playoffs again. Boston was careful with his innings in 2002, but Pedro was fabulous when he pitched and the Sox were really good, though 93 wins weren’t enough to make the playoffs that year.**

  

**The Sox were careful not to overwork their ace in 2003, and he was again the best pitcher in baseball as Boston took the wild card. Pedro was solid in an extra-innings Game 1 loss and solid in a Game 5 clinching win in the ALDS against Oakland. He gave up four runs in Game 3 of the ALCS against the Yankees, giving New York a 2-1 series lead. But the loss was overshadowed by an incredibly strange run-in Pedro had with Yankee bench coach Don Zimmer in the fourth inning, after Roger Clemens threw a pitch somewhere near Manny Ramirez:**

[**https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Lgn\_Q65xcxc\&ab\_channel=MadeForBaseball**](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Lgn_Q65xcxc&ab_channel=MadeForBaseball)

  

**Seems like Zimmer was real mad that Pedro drilled Karim Garcia the previous inning, and interpreted a look from Pedro to Yankees catcher Jorge Posada as “you’re next.” Zimmer seemed pretty embarrassed about the whole thing afterward, especially with a little distance: “He didn’t do nothing bad to me. If I come at you like a bulldog, you’re going to do something to pull me down -- do something, and that’s just what he did. I would’ve done the same thing if somebody came after me like that.” But New York mayor Michael Bloomberg was quoted saying, “You just cannot assault people, even if it’s on a baseball field,” and Pedro received death threats from Yankee fans.**

  

**So this, along with the Red Sox’ 85-year World Series drought, was the lay of the land when Pedro took the mound in New York to start Game 7 against Roger Clemens a few days later. Boston jumped out to a 4-0 lead, knocking Clemens out of the game in the fourth. Pedro pitched seven strong innings and, heading into the bottom of the eighth, the Sox led 5-2. After getting the first out, the Yankees strung together four straight two-strike hits to tie the game.**

  

**In the years since, much blame has been assigned to Boston manager Grady Little for leaving Pedro out there so long. Pedro was the best pitcher in baseball, and his last pitch was a 95mph fastball, 0-2, off the inside corner that Posada was lucky to bloop over the infield. Maybe the fan base is right and he should’ve come out of the game, but if the season’s on the line and Pedro’s still hitting 95, I’m sticking with him, no matter how fresh Alan Embree is.**

  

**Mariano Rivera entered the game for New York and threw shutout innings in the 9th, 10th, and 11th. The first pitch of the bottom of the 11th was a Tim Wakefield knuckleball, and Aaron Boone hit it out. The Yankees lost in the World Series to Florida.**

  

**A month later, Boston had traded a couple of young arms for Curt Schilling, who was probably the best pitcher in baseball outside of Pedro and was on the last year of his deal in Arizona. Both took a small step back that season, but the Sox won 98 games and the wild card, then swept the Angels in the divisional series to set up an ALCS rematch against New York. Pedro pitched OK but lost Game 2 in New York, where the fans had not forgotten a late-season interview after a loss where Pedro had said, “I’ll just tip my hat and call the Yankees my daddy.” The series looked awfully bleak when the Sox entered the bottom of the 9th in Game 4, losing the series 3-0 and the game 4-3. But they stole that one, and then turned a mediocre Pedro start in Game 5 into a 14-inning victory. Pedro pitched a bad seventh inning in an easy Game 7 win, then had one great start as Boston swept St. Louis in the curse-breaking World Series.**

  

**And then Pedro left. His contract was up, and the Mets were the only team to offer four guaranteed years. His last brilliant season was 2005, and the Mets were mediocre. Near the end of the season, at age 33, he started having hip, calf, and toe issues, and he never really got fully healthy again. He married Carolina Cruz over the offseason - he’d met her through his charity work in 1998, when she was an undergrad at Boston College.**

  

**The leg issues kept him to about a half-season in 2006, when the Mets made it to Game 7 of the NLCS before losing to St. Louis. Then he got rotator cuff surgery. He made it back for five starts in September 2007, and was effective even if his fastball wasn’t the same. He tore his hamstring in his for start of 2008 and missed the next two months. That seemed to be the last straw. He got torched for the rest of the season after coming back in June. Nobody signed him to start the 2009 season, but midway through the year he signed his last contract with Philadelphia. He pitched well enough to make the playoff roster, and made two good postseason starts before giving up four runs in four innings in the Yankees’ World Series-clinching Game 6. The 2010 season came and went without any teams calling.**

  

**In retirement, he’s worked for years in what appears to be a limited role as a special assistant to the Red Sox front office, usually working with young pitchers, and, simultaneously, been a studio broadcaster. He spends much of the year in Monoguayabo.**

**\#3 Dazzy Vance**

**Hunting and fishing type, retired to Homosassa Springs, FL. Great storyteller. Had a Southern accent even though he was from Nebraska. Loved to drink beer and moonshine. "It's hard for a pitcher to outsmart a batter who doesn't think."**

  

**Could shoot a pistol through a beer bottle without breaking the sides and once hit a dime from 45 feet three times in a row.**

**​Very good golfer.**

**PHYSICAL DESCRIPTION: Big dude for his time at 6'2", 200. Windmill, overhand, delivery. In the early part of his career, he liked to cut slits in his white undershirt sleeves so they'd flutter and distract batters, though baseball eventually banned the practice.**

**FASTBALL: Outstanding. Definitely threw smoke. He believed in throwing every pitch at high effort.**

**OFF-SPEED: Legitimately great overhand curveball. Seems like he was largely a two-pitch pitcher, but would throw a knuckleball up there a couple of times a game, as well. Didn’t throw a changeup. “I noticed the slow ones got pickled a lot.”**

**CONTROL: Good, not elite. Stuff over command guy. Extremely wild in the minors and in his early major league cups of coffee.**

**DURABILITY: Battled some injuries in his day. Didn't really get going until he was in his 30s, partially because of medical issues. Missed a big chunk of one season in his prime, broke down some at the end like we all do.**

[**https://www.mlb.com/video/the-film-archive-dazzy-vance/c-13226365**](https://www.mlb.com/video/the-film-archive-dazzy-vance/c-13226365)

**Born in Iowa, grew up on a farm in Cowles, Nebraska. When he was a kid, a guy down the road used to show him pelts of animals he'd hunted and say, "Ain't it a dazzy?" and Vance adopted it. Only two kids in the neighborhood, so he and his brother would play one-on-one baseball. Worked on the farm growing up. Went to high school in Hastings from age 18 to 20. Played a little semipro ball in the fall of 1911 in Superior of the Class D Nebraska State League and again in the 1912 season. Made $100 a month. Got blood poisoning from a scratch he received while boxing and dropped from 180 to 140 in a month. Jumped to Hastings for 1914, dominated and was bought by Class A Western League St. Joseph (MO) in July.**

  

**Pirates gave him a shot to open 1915. Got a start, but walked five and hit a guy in 2 2/3 innings and got sent back to Missouri. Pirates sold him to the Yankees in July, 16 walks and 23 hits allowed in 28 innings.**

  

**Boxing to stay in shape before spring training in 1916, Dazzy hurt his right elbow. His arm was completely shot. The Yankees sent him to Columbus, where he barely pitched. ​A hometown doctor told him it would heal in five years, and it did. He spent most of the year going to specialists.**

  

**He married Edythe Carmony in March 1917. Dazzy spent time in Toledo and then Memphis and was mediocre. As was not unusual for Dazzy in this period, he was broke when the season ended. In 1917 in particular, he had to work in a Memphis cotton gin just to drum up enough money to get back to Nebraska. He ran a cigar store in Omaha in the offseason.**

  

**He pitched well enough in 1918 with Memphis to get 2 1/3 more innings with the Yankees - he gave up five runs on nine hits and two walks before being sent back to the minors.**

  

**For 1919 he was offered $250 a month by the Yankees but $300 by Sacramento of the Pacific Coast League, where he at least stayed basically healthy. In 1920 he was with Memphis and New Orleans. Dazzy really got a handle on his breaking ball around this time. “When I joined the Pelicans and got some of the salubrious air of the Crescent City into my system, I was fixed and I have been doing fair to middlin’ ever since.”**

  

**By 1921 his arm was better than ever and he pitched well for New Orleans. After the season, Brooklyn tried to sign New Orleans catcher Hank DeBerry, who said he wouldn't go unless the 31-year-old Vance came, too - he thought Dazzy made him look like a better catcher. Dodgers scout Nap Rucker had kinda liked Dazzy anyway, so they signed the pair.**

  

**20s Dodgers became known as the Daffiness Boys. p53 story about cutting holes in pillowcases and demanding the Braves' signals on a train. Robbie chapter - favorite sign was "never mind use your own judgment". Dazzy's one rule: "Raise all the hell you want, but don't get caught." p87 Jess Petty trying to apologize for getting caught and getting a sportswriter to write his apology. p88 story of Robbie instituting bonehead fines and having to pay the first one himself.** 

  

**1924 made $9000. Brooklyn was in the pennant race.**

  

**Held out for $22,000 to start 1925, signed March 11. Threw a no-hitter.**

  

**Rough start to 1926, Robbie tried to talk him into coming out of the bullpen. Had boils early in the year, arm problems that Daz attributed to neuritis. He was 20 pounds overweight. Got hit in the arm by a line drive July 12. Three men on third story p90**

  

**Weight back down by spring training 1927.**

  

**Made $20,000 in 1928, highest-paid pitcher in baseball. Another incredible season, at age 37, but the Robins had no offense and finished well out of the running. Missed the first few weeks of ‘29 with flu complications. Was still solid, but no longer unhittable when he came back. Brooklyn finished sixth for the fifth year in a row and Robinson started talking to the press about moving Vance. (And everyone else, too.) It seems like it wasn’t just the losing - Dazzy was getting on his nerves, though it’s not clear to me why. Started getting treatment for rheumatism after the season.**

  

**Near fatal bout of pneumonia in the late 30s.**

**\#4 Randy Johnson**

**Loves photography (studied photojournalism at USC and took pictures for the school paper), plays the drums**

**Kept to himself and rode around on a bike in college**

**In college stayed in a house with a roof too low for him to stand up in most of the room**

**Very intense on the mound, competitive**

**Scott Bradley, who caught Johnson in his first three years with the Seattle Mariners, said: "It bothered Randy that every other pitcher would go over scouting reports, and for some reason, because he was big and had control problems, they'd pretty much say, 'O.K., Randy, just throw strikes, just get the ball over.' "I think he was kind of self-conscious. If everybody else was going over scouting reports, he wanted to be a pitcher just like everybody else."**

  
  

**PHYSICAL DESCRIPTION: 6'10", 225 pounds, wild hair, left-handed with a low, wide release point. The ball came in from a completely different place against him than against anybody else in baseball history. Only Jon Rauch has been taller in the major leagues.**

**FASTBALL: Broke 100. Hardest thrower in baseball in the 90s. Dipped a little velocity-wise even as he became a better pitcher in the early 2000s, then really started falling off in the mid-2000s. Mostly relied on a four-seamer, but had a two-seamer/sinker that he'd play off it.**

**OFF-SPEED: Maybe the greatest slider of all-time. Could throw it 90+. Not entirely clear to me how anyone ever hit it. Developed a splitter later in his career that he used like a changeup, more about changing speeds than huge break.**

**CONTROL: Was truly wild in the minors and very bad in the majors up through 1992. In 1993, he upgraded to mediocre control, where he hovered until about 2000, when it got pretty good and he became unhittable. (That's how you peak at age 38 without steroids.)**

**DURABILITY: Really great, consistently threw a lot of innings and a lot of pitches and still pitched full major league seasons through age 42. Back surgery in 1996 and knee surgery in 2003 blew up those two seasons.**

  

[**https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=fI86bj0UfNs**](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=fI86bj0UfNs)

[**https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=1PyCpG06138**](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=1PyCpG06138)

**https://www. .youtube.com/watch?v=DYXTV51GdUs**

**Here's Randy Johnson joining a Diamondbacks broadcast, weirdly leading off with him inviting people to follow his Instagram photography:**

[**https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=uXRoXyfOKxE**](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=uXRoXyfOKxE)

**Randy grew up in the Bay Area and went to USC, where he was on the same team as Mark McGwire. (He also rode the bench for two years in basketball.) He was a fourth-round draft pick coming out of high school, but it was all still mostly projection - his mechanics were bad, so his control was terrible. His velocity was all over the place, too, partially because he would try to take something off the fastball just to get it over the plate. Even early in college he mostly threw in the upper 80s, but would randomly touch 95 and get coaches and scouts paying attention. After his sophomore year, he went on a trip to Taiwan with USA baseball, and at a dinner some of the Taiwanese players came up to him and asked, "How can you be so tall and throw so slow?"**

  

**Montreal drafted him in the second round after his junior year, 1985, and for the next three years he was somehow effective in the minors despite walking nearly a batter an inning. (It probably had something to do with all the strikeouts and dearth of hits.) He missed two months in 1988 after breaking his right hand punching a wall. He got called up later that season and was solid in four starts.**

  

**A month into the 1989 season, Johnson had walked 26 guys in 29 innings for the Expos. Even at age 25, on the heels of that April, he was considered an interesting enough prospect for Seattle to accept as the centerpiece of a deal for Mark Langston. Montreal had plans to be good in 1989, and they shipped five pitchers to the Mariners for five excellent months of Langston before he left in free agency. (The Expos went 81-81 in 1988, and they went 81-81 in 1989.) Randy made 22 mediocre starts for a bad Seattle team in 1989, his first consistent major league innings.**

  

**He was in the rotation all year long in 1990, and made more of a splash - he pitched about like he did the year before, and Seattle was still bad, but he threw a no-hitter (the franchise’s first) and snuck onto the All-Star team due to a fortunate 9-3 start. He led the league in walks.**

  

**Then, in 1991 & 1992, his control issues went to new heights. He walked over six hitters per nine innings each season, something only four or five other pitchers have ever been allowed to do. The strikeouts were starting to come in bunches, too, but, at the end of the 1992 season, he was 29, and it seemed like he was probably just a wild, hard-throwing, slightly above average major league pitcher.**

**\#5 Ty Cobb**

**Dug in right up to the plate against Walter Johnson, who was terrified of beaning hitters, would switch his stance at the last second to bait pitchers into throwing what he wanted, would dance off third if Hal Chase was playing first because he knew he could draw a throw****  
****Toured a vaudeville act, The College Widow, after the 1911 season**

**Interview between 1929 and 1930 World Series:** [**https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=rqnH5BrRWnk**](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=rqnH5BrRWnk)

**This bizarre video of Cobb on a game show in 1955, where none of the contestants recognize him:** [**https://youtu.be/5T5UNeByuJE**](https://youtu.be/5T5UNeByuJE) 

**PHYSICAL DESCRIPTION: Listed at 6'1, 175, tall for the time. Left handed hitter, famous for holding his hands apart on the bat.**

**HIT: Maybe best ever, though there’s an argument that one of the slower guys’ batting average accomplishments are more hitting-driven - Rod Carew, Ted Williams, Rogers Hornsby, Tony Gwynn, even Ernie Lombardi. But Cobb did lead the majors in batting average nine times from 1907-19.**

**POWER: Good. Never hit more than 12 homers in a season, and a great, lightning-fast hitter will run into some doubles. Frequently led the league in doubles and triples and ranked among the leaders in homers. The grip he used is definitely a bat-control-over-power grip (and with the dead ball, it's probably the right bet), so I'd believe that there was more power in there somewhere if he wanted to give up some contact hitting. On the other hand, the guy did play six full seasons in the 20s and he didn't hit any homers then, either, though his doubles didn't fall off as fast as his stolen bases.**

**RUN: Excellent. Led the majors in steals five times, though it should be noted that he also got caught plenty. Lots of triples. Fell off in his thirties like everyone. Extremely aggressive baserunner.**

**FIELD: Solid. Seems to have been above average, stayed about average as a centerfielder even as his speed tailed off.**

**THROW: Below average, though he played a fair amount of right field so it can't have been a disaster.**

**PLATE DISCIPLINE: Ranged from mediocre to good, strangely non-linearly. Was a pretty low walk guy through about 1912, peaked at 118 walks in 1915, dipped back down to 38 in 1919 (in 150 fewer PA, but still), and then was pretty good for most of the 20s.**

**​DURABILITY: Missed a lot of time in 1914 with a broken thumb. Out for chunks of 1918 and 1920, as well. Played full major league seasons at age 20 and 40, though, fifth all-time in plate appearances.** 

[**https://youtu.be/QsCwiX1k4b8**](https://youtu.be/QsCwiX1k4b8)

**In 1911, Cobb was in a parking lot and saw a 19-year-old jump into Cobb’s Chalmers sedan and drive off. Ty caught the car on foot, jumped into the front seat, and threw the kid out. In court the next day, after hearing the thief’s wife talk about how things had been going poorly for them, Cobb asked the judge to let him go.**
