**\#7 Barry Bonds**

**  
  
**

  

**More than anything else, I think Barry Bonds just really wanted everyone to like him. But he just went about it the wrong way, over and over and over. When he signed with the Giants, he asked to wear the \#24 that his godfather, Willie Mays, wore in San Francisco, even though it was retired. In his head, he was honoring Mays, but after an outcry, he switched to his father’s \#25,**

  

**he draped his arm around the two-year-old son of first baseman Dan Peltier and asked, “Hey Colton, who’s your favorite baseball player?” When Colton replied, “My dad\!” Bonds said dryly, “Why? He never plays.”**

**​**

**He took a nap in the middle of a high school all-star team tryout.**

  

**Definitely a showboat. Bragged about money he'd turned down. Bobby would show up drunk to practices and yell at the coaches from the stands. Skipped practices.**

  

**eExtremely high commitment to working out.**

  

**Got into a fistfight with Mike Carreon after making a comment about his wife. Afterward, apologized, and said Mike was one of his best friends on the team. Carreon was shocked because he didn’t like Bonds at all.**

  

**(For Bonds, the grudge didn’t die. In an interview with Playboy two years later, Bonds accused the Pirates of masterminding the altercation. “Why would a microphone and a TV crew be right there at that time?” he said. “Just to stir up shit. The funny thing is, my dad told me before I went to spring training, ‘They’re going to set you up when you get there.’ He was right.”) Had his dad's agent, Rod Wright, but left him right before hitting free agency.**

  

**Continued to work out with Greg Anderson in the thick of the scandal.**

  

**Always seemed to get along with kids. Was determined to be there for his son, Nikolai. Batboy.**

  

**He broke into a teammate's apartment and stole his mom's specialty green chili.**

  

**​Ripken had just played in his 2,131st consecutive game, snapping Lou Gehrig’s supposedly unbreakable record, and he was shaking hands with every fan within reach. “I could never reach out like that,” Bonds told Duca. “I’d be too scared. If you could hear the things people say to me from 50 yards away—I’ll never get that close to people. Never.”**

  

**He got into a clubhouse fight with Pittsburgh teammate Andy Van Slyke and a bunch of teammates jumped in, all on Van Slyke's side. Van Slyke came up for free agency after 1988, and Pittsburgh signed him to a three-year, $5.5M deal - Bonds was pre-arb and, when the Pirates offered him $60,000 above what they had to, Bonds was pissed. Really got upset after his first arbitration hearing. And his second, claiming that he wouldn't play for $100 million if he didn't get a $3M contract for 1991. GM Doughty even bashed Sun at the 1991 hearing, calling her a terrible influence. Barry lunged at him at one point, but was held back by his agent.**

  

**He was convinced teammates were using his Trans Am without permission and he started writing the odometer measurement on a piece of paper. (“Confession,” says Lopez. “We were. It was the nicest car around, and when he went home for Thanksgiving or Christmas we’d cruise around in that thing like rock stars.”)**

  

**On the first day of college practice, he parked his brand-new black Trans Am in the head coach's reserved parking space. Freshmen carried the team's bags off the bus, but Barry never did, and he made no secret about it.**

  

**In the minors he would receive packages of baseball gear from Bobby, but wouldn't give extras to his much-poorer teammates unless they paid for them. He cried at his locker when he didn't make the big club out of spring training in 1986.**

  

**As a rookie: "Move, motherfuckers,” Barry ordered. “Do I come to your house at suppertime and bother you? Now get the fuck out of my house at suppertime and when I’m done I’ll talk to you."**

  

**In 1987, bullpen coach Rich Donnelly was holding a clinic at a high school and asked if Barry would help. It snowed and Donnelly assumed Bonds wouldn't show,**

  

**On the morning of January 18, 1987, the coach woke up, looked out the window, and cursed. It was snowing heavily, which meant the youth baseball be missing one key ingredient—Barry Bonds. One month earlier, Donnelly had asked Barry to participate and was shocked to hear the young outfielder reply, “Absolutely.” But with snowflakes blurring the landscape, there was no way Barry would show. Not for a measly $500 appearance fee and an audience of 25 rural kids. Donnelly arrived at the school at 8 A.M. , hoping at least for a courtesy call from Barry. Instead, he received shocking news. “Rich, everything’s fine,” said the school’s principal. “We’ve got coffee and donuts, and one of your players is already here.” Donnelly expected it to be pitcher Stan Fansler or outfielder Trench Davis, but upon entering the gymnasium he spotted Barry, standing in the middle of a circle of coaches and telling one uproarious Jim Brock story after another. Once the kids showed up, Barry stayed for four hours, doling out autographed bats and gloves. Before leaving he tapped Donnelly on the shoulder and handed him the $500 check. “Rich, do me a favor,” he said. “Keep this and write it over to the athletic fund here.”**

  

**Showed up at John Cangelosi's house and said, "Let's buy motorcycles\!" Reliever Ted Power signed with Pittsburgh, and was going to stay at the team motel in Florida until Bonds insisted he move into his apartment, rent-free.**

  

**He tended to like friends he could humiliate without loss of loyalty.**

  

**Bonds arrived wearing a black vest with large white splotches, and as he crossed the room Williams called out, “What’d you do, run into a cow on the way to the ballpark?” This was typical clubhouse ribbing. “You old fuck\!” Bonds screamed. “What the fuck do you know about fashion?” “It was,” says Nevius, a witness to the exchange, “a psycho-killer moment.”**

  

**On a Sunday in mid-March, Padres outfielder Phil Plantier was hogging a batting cage inside Scottsdale Stadium when Bonds politely asked him to leave. “Fuck off\!” shouted Plantier. “I’m in here.” Bonds flipped. “You can’t talk like that in my house\!” he said. “I’ve been in this league too long to take that shit from a punk-ass like you.” Bonds swatted Plantier across the face with the back of his hand. “Pimp slapped him like a little bitch,” recalls John Patterson, a Giants second baseman. “Just punked the shit out of Phil Plantier.”**

  

**Big excuses guy. An all-time classic: Kelly Stinnett hit a fly ball over Bonds's head, and Barry just turned and stared at it. Only then it didn't leave the park, and Bonds had to chase it down. After the game, he said that, while he was watching the ball, he was thinking about the impact his divorce was having on his kids.**

  

**Talk about Kimberley Bell here? Liz Watson marriage a business arrangement p208?**

  

**Was a jerk to support staff and rookies. “Personally, I hope Barry dies,” says Pete Diana, the former team photographer. “I hate him that much. I’ve seen him do stuff to people that goes beyond evil. I’ve seen him walk down a hallway and throw a whole bag of sunflower seeds at a security guard. I’ve seen him ride clubhouse employees to death and tip them close to nothing.”**

  

**​“I don’t think Bobby was doing it intentionally, but he really got to Barry,” says Reynolds. “Barry would hit a home run and drive in four, and Bobby would not let him get the glory. Bobby would have to talk about the game when he hit two home runs and had five RBIs.”**

  

**Asked to sit out of a game because of a headache. Leyland was furious. Then he found out the reason was because Barry was out dancing until 6 AM.**

  

**Called a young female reporter over so that he could flip through a Playboy with her. Would tell female reporters strangely detailed information about his sex life.**

  

**Some charities he worked with said the guy came by consistently and was wonderful. Away from the spotlight, teammates agreed little had changed. At one point during the season the Giants were visiting Colorado when Bonds was tapped on the shoulder by a Rockies clubhouse employee. “I’m friends with Brian Fisher,” he said. “Remember? He was a pitcher with you on the Pirates.” Bonds sighed dismissively, but continued to listen. Fisher’s son Kyle had been diagnosed with cystic fibrosis (he died the following year), and Brian held an annual pool tournament fund-raiser to fight the disease. Bonds was well aware of the boy’s condition. “Brian was wondering if you’d sign a couple of posters and balls that they could raffle off,” the clubhouse staffer asked. “A lot of guys have contributed—Larry Walker, Randy Johnson, Steve Reed…” Bonds scowled. “Fuck you,” he said. “And fuck Brian Fisher.”​**

  

**He and Orel Hershiser brought in new exercise machines to San Francisco's weight room - anyone could use Hershiser's, nobody could use Bonds's.**

  

**Bonds told Danny Darwin's nephew that he didn't like kids and tore up the baseball card he wanted signed. In an entirely separate incident, got into a fistfight with Darwin after he didn't go hard after a line drive.**

  

**Wanted to be a Hollywood type, hung around with Eddie Murphy, Sugar Ray Leonard, Arsenio Hall.**

  

**​Did not get along with Dusty or Leyland.**

  

**Backed Republican candidates. Favorite musicians are Barbra Streisand, Kenny G., Michael Bolton, and Celine Dion. Liked to claim he was from LA because he thought it sounded cooler.**

  

**With Bonds nearing his 600th career home run, Chronicle photographer Deanne Fitzmaurice asked the superstar whether he would allow her to shoot his off-the-field life for a special package. Surprisingly, Bonds agreed. Over the course of a week, one moment stood out in Fitzmaurice’s mind. On a lazy afternoon, Barry and Liz attended daughter Aisha Lynn’s gymnastics class. “He’s just loving it,” recalls Fitzmaurice. “He’s smiling, laughing, relaxed.” In the middle of an exercise, a young boy asked Bonds for an autograph. “Sorry,” Bonds said. “I’m watching my daughter.” When the child began to cry, Bonds said, “OK, OK. Come on over.” Chaos ensued. Everyone in attendance—children, teenagers, adults—swarmed the baseball star with markers and scraps of paper. “That’s it\!” Bonds said. “I’m out of here.” “It was just sad,” Fitzmaurice says. “All he wanted to do was watch his daughter.”****  
**[**https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=k8YriBpQkVA****  
****  
**](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=k8YriBpQkVA)**PHYSICAL DESCRIPTION: ​Bonds is 6'2, and he went from 185 or so as a young player up to about 230 at his peak. He comes up as a twig, but he's definitely put on some weight by 1992, and by 1996 he's pretty big. Reaches full, steroid-aided size around 2000.****  
****HIT: Excellent in the early 2000s, won a couple batting titles. Before that, merely good, hovered around .300 while he had good speed.**

**POWER: Elite at his peak, four straight years leading the league in slugging and did hit 73 homers in 2001.**

**​**

**Here's a great highlight reel of Bonds hitting absolutely monstrous home runs at a bunch of different stages of his career. (There's also a vaguely heartbreaking moment around the 5:20 mark, after Bonds's record-breaking 756th homer. After \#755, he met 16-year-old Nikolai at the plate with a hug, but after \#756, Nikolai goes in for the hug and is largely ignored by his dad.)****  
****RUN: Very good in his 20s, still stole 28 bases at age 33. By the time he was at his best, his speed was largely gone.**

**FIELD: One of the better defensive left fielders ever early in his career, declined with his speed. Average in his early 30s, pretty bad at his hitting peak.**

**ARM: Awful. The biggest reason he was one of the best defensive left fielders ever is that his arm wasn't playable in center field.**

  

**Bonds defensive highlights - cut into this 1992 interview:****  
****PLATE DISCIPLINE: Impossible. Walked 232 times in 2004, 62 more times than any other player has ever walked in a season. While his plate discipline was certainly outstanding, you can't get to that level on batting eye alone - he drew an outrageous 120 intentional walks in 2004, and he was certainly not getting pitches to hit in every other at-bat. (Greg Maddux had an awesome line where he claimed Bonds at his peak was the easiest hitter in the world to pitch to: "Because if it mattered, you just walk him.") My point here is just that, while it's hard to pick someone else as the greatest-ever batting eye, I think there's an argument for someone like Eddie Stanky, who walked well over 100 times even though nobody feared him as a hitter. The intentional walk thing is pretty unbelievable, though, on its own terms - he drew twice as many intentional walks as anyone else ever, and less than 100 players have been intentionally walked as often in their careers as he was in 2004 alone. All that said, his teammates were always amazed by the quality of his swing decisions and ability to predict what pitch he would be thrown.**

**DURABILITY: Excellent. Really only had two significant injuries - a triceps issue in 1999 that limited him to 102 games, and the knee issues (at age 40) in 2005 that blew up his season.****  
****Barry was the son of another extraordinarily talented, personally difficult ballplayer. Bobby Bonds was the second player in baseball history to hit 30 homers and steal 30 bases in a season twice, and the first, Bobby's San Francisco teammate Willie Mays, was named Barry's godfather. (Bobby didn't make my player rankings, but he was a late cut.) Even so, he was traded six times while still a productive player​, due first and foremost to issues with his drinking - he became known for showing up at the ballpark hungover or still drunk, and had repeated driving incidents that were at least partially related.**

  

**Barry grew up in San Francisco, where Bobby played from 1968-74, and Barry stayed there as Bobby got traded throughout the major leagues. His dad was playing elsewhere from when Barry was 10 until his senior year of high school. He was an obvious first-round talent, even though teams considered him a potential character problem, arrogant and lackadaisical. Bobby, now without a job, decided to be Barry's advisor for the 1982 draft, and that broke ties on enough teams' draft boards to drop Barry into the second round, where he was picked by San Francisco. After negotiations between the Giants and Bobby stalled out, Barry went to Arizona State rather than sign.**

  

**Barry dominated, of course, but was a problem to deal with. After breaking curfew one too many times, coach Jim Brock held a players-only vote on whether Bonds should be kicked off the team. He was trying to make a point to the team that they needed Bonds to win the title they all were working toward, but the plan backfired when the players voted to get rid of him, and Brock was forced to backpedal and claim the vote had to be unanimous.**

  

**Pittsburgh took Bonds with the \#6 overall pick in 1985 (though at least two of the guys picked ahead of him were Barry Larkin and Will Clark), and he was in the majors for good by the end of May 1986. He led off and played centerfield for the rest of the year and was solid defensively but mediocre at the plate for a bad Pirates team.**

  

**The following season, he was moved to left field after the acquisition of Andy Van Slyke, and Bonds hit a little more, establishing himself as the best player in Pittsburgh and one of the 20 or so best position players in baseball. On a Montreal road trip at the beginning of June, Bonds went out to a strip club and met the bartender, 23-year-old Swedish immigrant Sun Branco. (Sun had dated more than a few ballplayers by this time, and was fairly well-known as a baseball groupie.) They eloped in Las Vegas six months later, which is even stranger considering that Branco claimed, in a later attempt to revoke their prenup, that she barely spoke English at the time.****  
****After 1988 & 1989 seasons where he improved again, Bonds was one of the ten best position players in baseball. Then in 1990, he exploded, right as the Pirates were rounding into shape. Bonds hit .300 for the first time, with 33 homers and 52 steals - the only 30 homer, 50 steal season in baseball history. He was the best player in baseball besides Roger Clemens and Rickey Henderson. For Pittsburgh, Doug Drabek had an excellent year on the mound, and Bonds's outfield mates, Bobby Bonilla and Van Slyke, helped Barry lead the Pirates to the playoffs.**

  

**The Pirates lost in six games to Cincinnati, in a series kept fairly uninteresting by the Reds' "Nasty Boys" bullpen of Rob Dibble, Norm Charlton, and Randy Myers. (I've read that the name comes from Janet Jackson's "Nasty", but for the life of me I can't figure out why.) Bonds hit terribly, as did the rest of the Pittsburgh starting lineup. The 1991 season was similar - excellent regular season for Bonds and the Pirates, and then Bonds did nothing in a first round playoff exit, this time in seven games to Atlanta.**

  

**He exploded just in time for free agency in 1992, reaching a level that would've placed him in the top 25 all-time even without his early 2000s seasons. He led the majors in OBP and slugging, stole 39 bases, and won a Gold Glove. All season long, even as the Pirates won their division, he was talking about leaving. Barry gave a midseason interview discussing potential free agent destinations.**

  

**Pittsburgh made it to the playoffs, and Barry started the series against Atlanta 1-for-11. He was getting destroyed in the press for demanding this huge payday while never coming through in the playoffs. Bonds hit well in the final three games of the series, but his final play as a Pirate would not be a happy memory.**

  

**Pittsburgh took a 2-0 lead into the bottom of the ninth of Game 7, when Atlanta put together a rally. With two outs, the Braves cut the deficit to one run and loaded the bases with the pitcher's spot coming up. Backup catcher Francisco Cabrera (who had 12 major league plate appearances all season) pinch hit and singled to left, bringing across one run easily, and the second run in a close play after a typically weak throw to the plate from Bonds:**

**Barry was devastated, and was not the only Pirate to cry at his locker postgame.**

  

**Bonds entered free agency with a list of eight teams, only to find that half of them didn't want to offer him a contract. San Francisco, not on the list but with new ownership trying to make a splash, signed him to a record six year, $43.75M deal, the equivalent of about $13M a year today. He was never the highest-paid player in baseball, though, first trailing former teammate Bobby Bonilla, who made more than $6M in 1993 and 1994, despite never coming remotely close to Bonds's level as a player. (By the time Bonds's raise kicked in for the 1995 season, Detroit's Cecil Fielder had been signed to a $9M per year deal.)**

  

**Things didn't start smoothly in San Francisco. The first day of spring training, he walked over to a group of ten pitchers and, bizarrely, said, to each in turn, “I own you. I own you. I own you. I own you.” Obviously, this went over poorly. Then, in an exceptionally uncomfortable chain of events, Bonds first wore \#24, the previously-retired number of his godfather, Willie Mays, before public outcry scared him into switching to his backup plan, his father's \#25. After four years of sobriety, Bobby Bonds was hired as the Giants' first base coach.**

  

**Barry had another all-time season in 1993, and the Giants leapt from a 72-90 record before Bonds's arrival to 103-59, losing the division to Atlanta on the last day of the season. Barry took a small step back in strike-shortened 1994, but the Giants fell quite a ways after Will Clark signed with Texas, Robby Thompson struggled, and the pitching declined.**

  

**Barry's relationship with Sun fully deteriorated around this time. It had never been great - Sun wanted to go to work, and to have a stronger voice in the relationship, which Barry seemed to take as an affront to his manhood. Early on, in a truly strange compliment, he described her as having "more patience than toilet paper. Toilet paper just sits there and waits." Barry slept around a lot on the road, though it seemed that there was some sort of understanding between them. There was a domestic incident that culminated in Sun calling the police, then begging them to leave when they mentioned that the incident would probably be in the newspapers. (During later legal proceedings, she would state that there were multiple instances of physical abuse.)**

  

**​But the last straw was when Barry's relationship with Jennifer Peace, a 23-year-old porn star who went by Devon Shire, became very public on the heels of a paternity suit. (It was eventually demonstrated that the child was not Bonds's.) Barry filed for divorce not long after. The legal proceedings lasted six years, were long, drawn-out, and nasty, and certainly seemed to take a toll on him.**

  

**The Giants were bad again in 1995 and awful in 1996, and they started shopping Bonds around. Other teams came back with real offers - Bonds was considered a clear positive, even with that huge contract and difficult reputation - but nothing like what you'd expect for a guy who was still the second-best player in baseball (behind Ken Griffey, Jr.). San Francisco took Bobby off the field and made him a part-time scout, and Barry reacted by demanding a trade, not realizing that management was already trying. Management eventually decided that the offers they were getting for their other high-salaried player, Matt Williams, were preferable, and sent Williams to Cleveland for three mediocre players, one of whom was second baseman Jeff Kent. Bonds eventually signed a contract extension around $10M a year for the 1999 and 2000 seasons, with a club option for 2001. And San Francisco got it into his contract that he needed to represent the team better publicly.**

  

**Bonds continued to be one of the top players in the game in 1997, and new additions Kent, first baseman J.T. Snow, third baseman Bill Mueller, and starters Kirk Reuter and Shawn Estes all had excellent seasons, propelling San Francisco to the top of a weak division and the playoffs. When the Giants clinched the division, Barry was hugging fans, dancing, spraying champagne, crying. But the wild card Florida Marlins swept them in three games in the division series, and Bonds struggled in the postseason again.**

  

**Barry married Liz Watson in 1998, another Canadian former exotic dancer who had dated her share of pro athletes. (Their relationship had begun before Sun, and didn't exactly stop during the marriage.) Barry had a very good year, not a great year, in 1998. It was the year of the great chase to break Roger Maris's season record of 61 homers, and Bonds was openly annoyed at all the positive attention Mark McGwire and Sammy Sosa were getting with each home run, not least of all because he suspected they were on steroids. He started working with now-infamous trainer Greg Anderson, who eventually got him using Stanozolol, Deca-Durabolin, and HGH. His power numbers nudged up a little through the 1999 season, but his on-base percentage took a big dip and he had his worst season in a decade. He also missed almost two months early in the season with a ruptured triceps tendon, an injury likely brought on by overly-fast muscle growth.**

  

**Bonds was back to being one of the best players in baseball in 2000 as the Giants turned into a 97-win team. Kent won the MVP, though Bonds was certainly the better player. Barry noticed. It seems like theirs was a relationship doomed to fail from the start, but this slight didn't help. San Francisco lost in four games in the NLDS, and Bonds struggled at the plate yet again.**

  

**Giants signed Eric Davis and Shawon Dunston, who checked Bonds, for the 2001 season. Started being a helpful teammate. Before 2001, starting working with Victor Conte at BALCO. THG (The Clear). Home run record. Bought Shawon Dunston a fully-loaded Mercedes, even though Dunston says the bet started as a joke. Came up for free agency after the season but found the market tepid at age 37, wound up taking a five-year, $90M contract from San Francisco that far outstripped other offers. Weighed 228 during the year.**

  

**In the middle of the 2002 season, Kent lit into third baseman David Bell and Bonds got in Kent's face about it:****  
****Bonds publicly asked management to resign Kent.**

  

**Finally had the first good postseason series of his career, and his first playoff series win, as the Giants beat Atlanta in five games in the division series, then played well again as they beat St. Louis easily to head to the World Series.**

  

**Legendary video of Bonds homering in Game 2, followed by Tim Salmon saying, "That's the farthest ball I've ever seen hit."****  
****Game 6 was over, the Giants would win. Disaster strikes. Video of Bonds error.Barry was absolutely dominant in the series.****  
****No-punishment steroid testing began with the 2003 season. Bonds was, again, by far the best player in baseball, this time at age 38. Weak playoff series as they lost to Florida in four. BALCO was raided at the end of the 2003 season with Bonds immediately linked.**

  

**Baseball announced before the 2004 season that a fourth positive steroid test would result in a year's ban. Ridiculous season. In November went before a grand jury and claimed he hadn't used. Likely perjured himself.**

  

**Three right knee operations between November and March. An article came out in the San Francisco Chronicle about his relationship with Kimberley Bell. Bonds was just defeated, sick of steroid stories already. A fourth surgery in May. Only wound up playing the last two weeks of the season, likely because he was at 703 homers and 41.**

  

**His first full season post-injury, he still wasn't the same guy he was from 2001-2004. He still led the league in OBP, but he had his worst power season since Pittsburgh. But Bonds still had enough to get past Ruth on the home run list and get within striking distance of Henry Aaron. The Giants were mediocre.**

**\#8 Honus Wagner**

**Loved hunting and fishing**

**Mostly wore unfashionable clothes (jeans and corduroys in fancy hotel lobbies)**

**Didn't like fancy things in general, more ham-and-eggs type**

**Would join in with a kids' streetball game**

**Excellent pool player, worked in a pool hall as a teenager**

**Liked going to the square dances when playing for Louisville**

**PHYSICAL DESCRIPTION: Seems like every description of Wagner ever written includes that he was "barrel-chested," as well as "bow-legged." Big, thick dude for a shortstop. 5'11, 200. Long arms, would stand far away from the plate in a wide stance. Separated his hands on the bat, copying Cap Anson.**

**HIT: Great. Eight batting titles. Though it's worth noting that as a pure batting average hitter, even in his time he wasn't quite in the range of Cobb or peak Lajoie.**

**POWER: Very good. Normally in the range of 5-10 homers a year, which was no joke in the 1900s. Led the league in doubles every year and triples more than once; in 1900 hit 45 doubles and 22 triples, a combination only he, Cobb, and Adam Comorosky (?) have achieved in a season.**

**RUN: Elite. Tons of steals and triples. Certainly one of the better athletes in baseball when he played, though he wasn't super fluid doing it.**

**FIELD: Above average. The numbers see him as a clear positive defensively, even when his legs started to go in his late 30s. That said, he was probably never the best shortstop in baseball in the field. Used a tiny glove with a hole in the palm - he said the ball stuck better in your hand.**

**THROW: Really good arm. Played him in right field in the early days. Pitched a few innings in 1900 and in 1902 and didn't embarrass himself. Teammate Tommy Leach said, evocatively, "He just ate the ball up with his big hands, like a scoop shovel, and when he threw it to first base you'd see pebbles and dirt and everything else flying over there along with the ball."**

**PLATE DISCIPLINE: About average, maybe a little better. Typically 50-60 walks a year when he was good.**

**​DURABILITY: Outstanding. Played 140 games a year into his 40s, all-time leader in games played upon his retirement.**

**Best video of Honus I can find is of him screwing around as a white-haired old man:**

[**https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=N34UT6VIPYE**](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=N34UT6VIPYE)

**Interview:** [**https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=YiiwBFD-5vk\&ab\_channel=King.\_**](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=YiiwBFD-5vk&ab_channel=King._)**.**

  

**Son of German (then Prussian) immigrants. Father was a coal miner. Born Johannes in Pittsburgh in 1874. Hans briefly worked in the coal mine as well at age 12. (Coal in the area was normally being taken from the surface of a hill rather than a mine shaft.) Worked three months at a steel mill at 18. At 21 he was working in one older brother's barber shop, while another brother, Al, was playing local semipro ball and found Honus a few opportunities. (Al is listed as “Butts” Wagner in many places, though I’ve struggled to figure out why.) Over the next couple of years, he jumped from team to team in western PA, Ohio, and Michigan. He was offered a $100 contract with the Pirates in 1896, but passed on it when he realized they would be sending him to play in Kansas City.**

**Even in 1897, when Wagner was dominating the Atlantic League for the Paterson, N.J., team, there are a surprising number of quotes from contemporary scouts describing him as “awkward,” “clumsy,” and bow-legged, and thus uninteresting as a prospect. Pittsburgh and Louisville were the two National League clubs who went after him, and Louisville won the bidding war in July with $2,000. Honus liked Paterson and seemed legitimately unsure whether he wanted to go to Louisville, particularly when he learned he would probably be coming off the bench, but was eventually convinced.**

**At 23, Honus was a year younger than his manager, Fred Clarke. After playing all over the place in the minors, mostly third base for Paterson in 1897, Wagner pretty quickly became the Colonels’ regular center fielder and one of the better players on a bad team.**

**He signed for $1500 for the 1898 season (about $47,000 today). Fined $5 for missing a team meeting. Manager Clarke said, “Honus, like others, was inclined to sow his wild oats. I took him to task one day in 1898 and offered him some advice. He gave me his word and promise...he never broke \[it\].” Al played his only year in the bigs, spending much of the year with Washington before ending it with Brooklyn. Playing mostly first and third, Honus solidified himself as a major leaguer but wasn’t quite as good as he’d been the year before. (Although ten homers put him second in the National League.) He liked his first baseman’s glove and used it at third as well - strange, certainly, though the gloves weren’t as different as they are today. Honus was consistently described as an awkward but successful fielder, and I’m sure the first baseman’s mitt was part of that in Louisville.**

**\#11 Mickey Mantle**

Mob boss daughter story

Joked that he led the league in crabs six times

Mantle & Martin: “The Yankee clubhouse was, like, below street level. We had windows like where people are walking along. Girls used to come stand there, and we used to shoot water guns up in their puss. We could see ‘em kinda flinch. They’d be looking around trying to figure out where the fuck that water’s coming from.” They once destroyed a hotel room by shooting Roman candles at each other.

Here's Mantle talking to David Letterman in 1985, featuring some Mantle hitting the town stories:

<https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=fkKB54NS0kQ>

  

PHYSICAL DESCRIPTION: Listed at 5'11, 200. Strong and athletic. Looked like a baseball player.

HIT: Very good. During his two superhuman seasons in 1956 and 1957, he won a batting title at .353 and lost another hitting .365. Playing in Yankee Stadium in the 50s and 60s makes his batting averages look pedestrian, but sitting on .300 for much of the rest of his career was no easy task.

POWER: Outstanding. His longest home runs are legendary, and it does seem like he was hitting more balls in the realm of 500 feet than anyone at his time or before. Very few players had more impressive power flashes, and it's worth remembering that he was in a tough offensive environment here, as well.

RUN: Ridiculous before the injuries, stayed pretty good through about age 30. His stolen base numbers don't jump off the page, but the fifties were essentially the low point of the stolen base throughout baseball history, and he placed in the top ten in the AL in steals seven times despite never getting more than 21.

FIELD: Solid. More athlete than anything else. Fielding numbers definitely drop off around age 30.

THROW: Good before his 1958 shoulder injury, then below average. You can find people claiming that his arm was really great, but it's tough to know if that's just because he's Mickey Mantle.

​PLATE DISCIPLINE: Excellent. Led the league in walks consistently, though he led the league in strikeouts consistently, as well.

​DURABILITY: Really not that bad. His left knee was a disaster, and he had a couple of collision injuries in his career, but he really only missed like a month total from 1952-1961. For a guy who was pretty beat up, he played an awful lot, and at a high level.

  
  

Highlights:

<https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_2lgYgio6Ok>

  

On Martin’s 29th birthday (in 1957), he, Mantle, Bauer, Whitey, Yogi, and their wives went to go see Sammy Davis, Jr. at the Copacabana Club. They got into a brawl in Davis’s defense. First public sense of Mantle’s partying. Martin traded after, at least partially because he was a bad influence on Mickey. (Mantle said that the bad influence was really Ford.)

**\#12 Rogers Hornsby**

PHYSICAL DESCRIPTION: Listed at 5’11, 175, pretty good size for a middle infielder of his era.

HIT: Elite. Has a pretty good argument as the greatest hitter for average of all time. Hit .402 over a five-year stretch from 1921-25, something nobody else has ever done. Ross Barnes has a .398 stretch, Ty Cobb has a .397 stretch, they’re the only guys who really even came close. And Cobb’s batting average was much more speed-driven, particularly at that time, than Hornsby’s ever was. Incredible line drive hitter.

POWER: Solid for most of his career. Consistent doubles power. Had three seasons (1922, 1925, and 1929) where he flashed major home run power - only hit 26 homers in his fourth-highest season but led the majors with 39 in 1922 and 42 in 1925. Only guy other than Ruth to lead the majors in homers in the 1920s.

RUN: Probably a little above average as a young player. Definitely took a step back after his 1923 knee issues.

FIELD: Up for debate, but certainly not sterling. There’s a consistent line that he couldn’t catch pop-ups, which is weird because, well, (1) everyone can catch pop-ups, (2) I’ve never heard that said about another major league player, and (3) his fielding numbers should be really horrendous if that’s true, and they aren’t. The fielding numbers for Hornsby in his early 20s, particularly when he was playing a lot of shortstop and third base, are pretty solid, tapering off to average after that. That still seems a little optimistic to me, but I can’t say it would shock me if an average-fielding well-known asshole got a bad defensive reputation.

THROW: Had to be pretty solid if he was spending that much time at short and third early on.

​PLATE DISCIPLINE: Well above average. The only guys with a higher five-year OBP than his 1921-25 number of .474 are Bonds, Ruth, Ted Williams, John McGraw, and Billy Hamilton. As a young player he was just OK, but around when his speed started going he really started drawing a lot of walks.

​DURABILITY: Very good through age 33 - had the knee injury in 1923 but that was about it. In 1930, he broke his ankle and only played 42 games. In 1931, he played just enough to prove he could still hit, but that was the last time he got any real at-bats. Boils on his feet were the final straw.

**\#14 Sandy Koufax**

**His ambition, as stated beneath his picture in the June 1953 Lafayette Legend yearbook, was “TO BE SUCCESSFUL AND MAKE MY FAMILY PROUD OF ME .”**

  

**Asked his Columbia professor's permission to leave early to go to the 1955 Dodgers World Series party.**

  

**​“He looked like Michelangelo,” Ernie Banks said. “Pitching, walking, whatever he did was kind of in rhythm with life, stylish .”**

  

**Bicoastal. Brooklyn boy who started in Brooklyn and ended the first star of LA sports. Brooklyn ownership dreamed of a Jewish star. Concentration camps were during his lifetime, remember. He and Maury Wills would screen each other's hate mail and make jokes about it.** 

  

**Not quite as stuffy as his reputation. Certainly hit the town in college, the kids at the camp where he was counselor loved his dirty jokes. Was followed around by groupies by day 1.**

  

**Kind, reserved, had a sense of humor. Gentle. Didn't like the limelight. Bus stop story p59?**

  

**PHYSICAL DESCRIPTION: Lefty listed at 6'2, 210. Strong. Reared way back on his windup. Pushed off the top of the rubber rather than off the side of it to get better leverage - would hook it with the front of his cleats. (Eventually taught this trick to Pedro Martinez when Pedro was a young Dodger.) Super fluid, natural delivery. All-around athlete. Tipped his pitches pretty badly in the stretch - he'd open his glove wide on the curveball - but it didn't seem to make much difference. Willie Mays: “I knew every pitch he was going to throw and I still couldn’t hit him.”**

**VELOCITY: Outstanding. Right alongside the top fastballs of his day. Lots of rise. There are a few people who claimed the ball re-accelerated when it got about fifteen feet from the plate, which is obviously ridiculous, but it does speak to the ridiculous amount of backspin he got on his four-seamer.**

**OFF-SPEED: Unbelievable curveball, one of the best ever. Big and slow, about as much movement as you ever see on a pitch, pretty close to 12-to-6 drop on the clockface. Would every once in awhile would take off a ton and throw a super-slow curveball, there's one in the highlight reel below. Huge hands, enabled him to gripp the curveball with his thumb out of the way of the ball. Solid changeup, mixed in a forkball late in his career.**

**CONTROL: Terrible through 1960. Put it together and made it passable in 1961 & 1962, got all the way to good for 1963-66.**

**DURABILITY: Troubled, to say the least. Missed a big chunk of 1962 with a finger injury, then missed a good bit of 1964 with the arthritic elbow that would end his career after the 1966 season. Had essentially a six-year peak due to injury, and missed half of two of those seasons with injury. He threw an awful lot of games and innings when he was right, though, cracking 300 innings three times.**

  

[**https://youtu.be/Gry6oHBFuKw**](https://youtu.be/Gry6oHBFuKw)

[**https://youtu.be/pfQB-6eeRzE**](https://youtu.be/pfQB-6eeRzE)

  

**Born Sanford Braun, birth father left when he was three and his mother remarried six years later. Parents were a CPA and a lawyer. Sandy Koufax grew up Jewish, though not particularly religous, in Brooklyn, parents wanted him to be an architect. Grew up next door to Buddy Hackett, two years behind Larry King at Lafayette High. Joe Torre was four years younger but played in the same sandlot games.**

  

**He was a basketball player first as a high schooler. In the infant days of the NBA, the Knicks played an exhibition game against Koufax the Lafayette High squad. Knicks star Harry Gallatin tried to do a couple of dunks to get the crowd going and misses his first two attempts. The high school coach went and grabbed Sandy and he hammered one home. Gallatin got Koufax's name and address on the way out, quoted in the paper saying he thought Koufax could make the NBA. (Although, again, an NBA where the best player on the Knicks misses wide open dunks.) Showed up to the University of Cincinnati without a scholarship, made the freshman basketball team and got himself one.**

  

**Ed Jucker coached both basketball and baseball and, looking to fill roster slots, Koufax volunteered. He wound up with 51 strikeouts and 30 walks in 31 innings. After a tryout at Ebbets Field, Brooklyn locked him up. He left Cincy in December to sign for $6000 a year with a $14,000 bonus, a large enough contract to guarantee Sandy would stay on the 25-man roster for all of 1955 and 1956 under the rules of the day. (Milwaukee and Branch Rickey's Pittsburgh Pirates offered him more money, but too late.) To make room, the Dodgers sent Tommy Lasorda to the minors.**

  

**Sandy, at 19, was making more money than some of his bench player teammates. ​Didn't help that he was Jewish. His first pitch in a spring training game went over the backstop. Some of his early teammates had issues with him because he was Jewish, the black players took care of him. Didn't help that he was not producing (or expected to produce) early on for a team that was competing. Wasn't playing anyway, but had a hairline fracture of his right ankle and missed a month. Debuted in late June, mostly just spot mop-up duty. Threw a complete-game, 14-strikeout shutout in August.**

  

**​Tried to study architecture at Columbia starting that fall, but eventually gave up. Didn't appear in the 1955 World Series, Brooklyn's first and only World Series title. After Game 7, he drove to Columbia for class.**

  

**Couldn't get consistent opportunities in 1956. Had a few relief opportunities early on, made the rotation for the month of  June, very sporadic after that. Brooklyn lost the World Series in seven to the Yankees, and Sandy again saw no action. Even in 1957, the Dodgers' last year in Brooklyn, the team was still pretty good and Sandy was too wild to really be a major cog in the machine. He was still 21 and mostly a spot starter, but that year he struck out 122 guys in 104 innings, making him the third major leaguer ever with more than a K per inning in that many innings. (The others were Herb Score in his two monster years of 1955 & 1956, and Bob Feller in 1937, when he was 18 and also had no idea where the ball was going.) Even so, he was wild enough, and the Dodgers' staff good enough, that it wasn't unreasonable to keep him out of the full-time rotation. Some minor arm troubles didn't help him crack it. He didn’t make it back to Columbia in the fall.**

  

**The move to Los Angeles aligned with a shift in the Dodgers' roster. Jackie Robinson retired after the '56 season, Roy Campanella after '57, and Pee Wee Reese had one bench season in LA before he followed suit. Don Newcombe was traded to Cincinnati early in the 1958 season. Their first year in LA, the Dodgers finished seventh.**

  

**In 1959, Don Drysdale broke out as a legitimate ace and they leapt to first. Sandy had two seasons where he bounced between the back of the rotation and long relief duty. The walks were still a major problem. L.A. won the 1959 World Series in six games over the White Sox, Sandy pitching seven strong innings in a Game 5 loss (and a successful two-inning relief stint in a blowout Game 1). In 1960, they finished fourth, and, despite throwing only 175 innings, Sandy finished second in the league in strikeouts and third in walks.** 

  

**In the immortal words of Willie Stargell, trying to hit Koufax was like “trying to drink coffee with a fork.”**

**​**

**By 1965, put Capsolin on his elbow before every game, which was essentially a hot sauce, as in made from chili peppers. Plus cortisone, Butazolidine, Empirin with codeine, then ice after.**

  

**Very reserved in retirement. Avoided the spotlight. Turned into something of a pitching guru, retired to Vero Beach near the Dodgers' spring training facility.**

**\#17 Walter Johnson**

**Probably the best person among the greatest of the great baseball players. Kindhearted, gentle, and thoughtful, he stands out starkly against the difficult personalities of his greatest contemporaries: Ruth, Cobb, and Hornsby. Johnson played his entire career for the Washington Senators, which for most of his tenure were managed or owned by Clark Griffith. On Johnson's deathbed in 1946, Griffith came to sit by his side and talk about the old days while his friend deteriorated.**

  

**Let's just say that that's a much better relationship than any of those other three had with their superiors.**

  

**\#\#\#BIG TRAIN, BARNEY (OLDFIELD?), BIG SWEDE**

**​**

**He was the kind of guy who, in an old-timers' game, had no problem throwing Babe Ruth meatballs so the Babe could try to hit one out. Johnson was very willing to sublimate his ego for the good of the show, not something all once-great athletes can do.**

  

**​Johnson exuded calm. All he really wanted to do was be outside, hang out with his dogs, and go hunting without a building in sight.**

  

**There's a layer of melancholy to Johnson that seems to lurk below all his quotes, though. And there are two events that had undeniable but understandable impacts in this way.**

**\#\#\#LETTER AFTER LOSING BABY**

**\#\#\#HAZEL'S DEATH**

**People who knew him say he was never quite the same after Hazel passed away. He'd been trying to get into managing, but after she died, his interest seemed to fade.**

  

**\#\#\#STORY OF MEETING HAZEL**

  

**\#\#\#CONGRESSIONAL RUN**

  

**You can hear him talk** [**here, calling the last Senators game of the 1939 season.**](https://sabrmedia.org/2015/07/14/this-is-what-walter-johnson-sounds-like-calling-a-senators-game-on-the-radio/)

**PHYSICAL DESCRIPTION: Johnson was a big guy for his time, listed at 6'1" and 200 pounds. He was a right-hander with an easy, fluid sidearm pitching motion.**

**VELOCITY: Elite. Maybe the greatest ever, for his time. There's just person after person who can't believe how hard he's throwing. A bunch of people have tried to figure out how hard that really was, which to me kind of feels like a fool's errand - I'd guess he was in the low 90s. Part of the appeal, it seems clear to me, is how little strain he seemed to be putting into the throw before it'd explode up to the plate. There's just quote after quote some old ballplayer saying he was the fastest ever, and then describing how he never even saw the ball when he struck out. One famous story describes him faking a pitch in the dark, while the catcher popped his glove, and the umpire called strike three.**

**CONTROL: Excellent from the beginning of his career through his peak. After his 1920 arm troubles, though, it took a big step back.**

**OFF-SPEED: Threw a curveball, relied on it more as his career went on. Looks like at his peak, he mostly ignored it. At any rate, it was just OK.**

**DURABILITY: Awfully impressive. In September 1908, with the Senators' pitching staff thin, Johnson started three straight games and threw shutouts in all of them. Had a sore arm in 1920 to the point he was worried his career might be over, but that's really the only year injuries derailed for him.**

[**https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=imwUHeuVqME**](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=imwUHeuVqME)

  

**Walter Johnson grew up on a Kansas farm before his family chased oil to southern California in his teenage years. He pitched for the oil company team and in nearby unorganized games until, at 18, he was signed by Tacoma of the Northwestern League for the 1906 season. He was released shortly after, but was picked up by Weiser of the Southern Idaho League, where he dominated for the next year and a half.**

  

**Weiser was a town of about 2000 people, well over a thousand miles away from the nearest major league team at the time. In the first few months of the 1907 season, Walter struck out 214 hitters in 146 innings and had a 0.55 ERA. During one hot streak, he threw 77 straight scoreless innings, including back-to-back no-hitters.**

  

**At the time, the way a major league team heard about a prospect was by a friend-of-a-friend wiring them a player's name, then sending a scout to go check him out. But it took awhile for anyone to go see Walter. Good young player, think he has a chance, playing for a decent minor league team in Baltimore - that's what a legitimate recommendation sounds like. Probably the hardest thrower of all-time, 77 straight scoreless innings, one of the best pitchers in baseball right now, 19 years old, playing in the middle of nowhere Idaho? That just makes your contact sound like an idiot, and, in 1907, it takes a week-long scouting trip to find out either way. Eventually, though, Washington catcher Cliff Blankenship got hurt and found himself on a train to Idaho, and Walter found himself with a contract offer from the Senators.**

  

**He shows up in August and it's immediately clear they've got a ballplayer on their hands. At 19, Walter has the third-highest strikeout rate in the majors, trailing only Philadelphia superstar Rube Waddell and the Giants' Red Ames. And there are whispers immediately about him being possibly the hardest thrower in baseball already. Washington finishes last in the American League.**

  

**By 1908, Johnson was Washington's ace. With the Senators well on their way to a second-to-last place finish, manager Joe Cantillon decided to test Walter's limits in early September. Walter threw a six-hit shutout on September 4, and, looking at his options for September 5, Cantillon figured his best chance was to just throw Johnson again. Four-hit shutout. Following a September 6 off-day, Walter started again in the first game of a September 7 doubleheader: Two-hit shutout. Cantillon was considering rolling him out again in the second half of the doubleheader, but Walter hid in the clubhouse until the game had started.**

  

**Johnson started the 1909 season late after a sickness that led to him losing 18 pounds, and he missed most of September with shoulder pain. In between he had the least-effective season of his youth, although he was still certainly the best pitcher on a Senators team that finished 42-110, last in the AL by 20 games.**

  

**He made his leap in 1910 - he'd be one of the best two pitchers in baseball for the next ten years running. He threw 370 innings and his strikeout numbers hit the roof. He came back to earth a bit in 1911, but only a bit. Washington finished seventh both seasons.**

  

**The next season, 1912, was when he went into the stratosphere. There's a pretty good argument that his next four seasons, 1912-15, all rank among the fifteen or so best pitching seasons in baseball history. And the Senators made a leap right along with him. Walter's roommate, centerfielder Clyde Milan, was turning into a pretty good ballplayer by this point, and rookie first baseman Chick Gandil (a few years away from spearheading the Black Sox scandal) was an upgrade. Walter and \#2 starter Bob Groom, who had a very good season, threw about half the team's total innings. Through the 41 previous years of openly professional baseball and nine incarnations of Washington franchises, the city had never had a team better than .500 - their best-ever finish was the 5th place 1871 Washington Olympics, who went 15-15. The 1912 Senators finished in second place, an unusually triumphant 14 games behind pennant-winning Boston.**

  

**But it was 1913 that was magical for Johnson. After a start on May 10, Walter was 8-0 with a 0.17 ERA, one run allowed in 53 2/3 innings. He'd already thrown four shutouts. By the time he gave up another run, he'd completed 55 2/3 consecutive scoreless innings, the record until Don Drysdale topped it in 1968 (and, eventually, Orel Hershiser topped Drysdale in 1988).**

**​**

**And that summer, he met Hazel.**

**\#24 Tom Seaver**

**PHYSICAL DESCRIPTION: Normal sized, fluid motion.**

**VELOCITY: Outstanding, though he was never all that close to being the hardest thrower in baseball. (Partially because Nolan Ryan had a stranglehold on that title.) Threw a four-seamer and a two-seamer.** 

**OFF-SPEED: Excellent slider. Had a curveball but didn't throw it much until later in his career. Didn't throw a true change-up until later on, either - said he threw three different speeds of fastball until he finally found the right change-up grip.**

**CONTROL: Very good early in his career. Walk numbers started climbing around 1975, and especially once he left New York, but it seems more accurate to call that increased nibbling than deteriorating control.**

**DURABILITY: Elite. Threw a ton of innings every year until his mid-30s, when he started having shoulder problems. Recovered to throw four more full seasons, retiring at 41.****

**\#26 Mike Schmidt**

**PHYSICAL DESCRIPTION: Listed at 6'2, 195. Tall, strong but not super muscular.**

**HIT: Average. Below average through 1979, above average after. Only hit .300 once.**

**POWER: Outstanding. Led the majors in home runs six times. The best power hitter of the 1980s and not far out of the conversation for the 1970s.**

**RUN: Pretty good early in his career, tailed off as he aged like everyone does. Legit athlete, though, played some shortstop early in his career and stole 29 bases in 1975.**

**FIELD: Outstanding. Won ten Gold Gloves and deserved them. Probably not the greatest defensive third baseman of all time, but, especially through about 1980, is in the conversation.**

**THROW: Very good arm - hard to be an elite defensive third baseman otherwise.**

**DURABILITY: Excellent. No major injuries while he was good enough to deserve playing time, lasted until he was 38. First decent season a hair on the later side at 24.****  
**[**https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=1C9jttsosP0****  
**](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=1C9jttsosP0)[**https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=tCZDE2yBzY4****  
**](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=tCZDE2yBzY4)[**https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=bqfPn5hWJsU****  
**](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=bqfPn5hWJsU)[**https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=09FVBE9InsM****  
**](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=09FVBE9InsM)**Mike Schmidt was born in Dayton, Ohio in 1949. He got hit by lightning climbing a tree at age four - not that that impacts his story at all, but it's good to keep track of these things. His dad ran a fast food joint called Jack's Drive-In, and his mom's family ran a local pool. Inevitably, Mike wound up working for both in his youth. He had surgeries on both knees in high school to repair football injuries, and was considered a physical specimen and a defense-first shortstop who couldn't seem to hit the ball consistently enough to put too much faith in his power. He wound up hitting .179 as a senior, which is about 400 points below what I would've guessed. Played basketball and football well until his knees made those sports untenable.**

  

**Schmidt showed up at Ohio University to study architecture and walked onto the baseball team. After a mediocre freshman year, he had a monster summer and came back a different player. Previously a switch hitter, he focused on the right side and was an All-American by his junior season. After his senior season, the Phillies took him in the second round of the 1971 draft. He spent a season and a half in the minors before being called up to Philly for 13 mediocre games late in 1972.****

**\#28 Jimmie Foxx**

**PHYSICAL DESCRIPTION: He was nicknamed "The Beast" and he earned it. Listed at 6'0", 195, which I'd guess is about right for his early Philadelphia days - definitely bigger than that in Boston.**

**HIT: Excellent. Won two batting titles and competed for more.**

**POWER: Elite. Retired with the most career homers in major league history excluding Ruth, and the most in a season (58 in 1932). Probably the best power hitter in the game for the whole decade of the 1930s. His three-year stretch from 1932-34 is as impressive compared to the league as any non-Ruth power stretch in baseball history.**

**RUN: Decent. Really not a bad athlete for a big, power-hitting first baseman. Set state record in sprints and the high-jump as a high schooler, played some soccer, basketball, and volleyball, as well. Doesn't show up much in the stats, but he at least stole ten bases a few times and hit a decent number of triples.**

**FIELD: A little above average in his 20s, a little below average in his 30s. Philly in 1935 and Boston in 1940 gave him 30ish games at catcher each, but he mostly stuck at first after 1928.**

**THROW: Very good arm, absolutely playable at catcher and third.**

**PLATE DISCIPLINE: Outstanding. Drew 100 walks regularly, consistently among the league leaders.**

**DURABILITY: Stayed very healthy through age 33, then things deteriorated quickly due to a combination of drinking, sinus and vision problems, and trouble with authority.**

**God this clip makes me laugh:****  
**[**https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=n7ufCEuM9ZY****  
**](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=n7ufCEuM9ZY)[**https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=yWWwaepGQig****  
**](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=yWWwaepGQig)**Interview:** [**https://www.nytimes.com/video/multimedia/100000002175079/audio-jimmie-foxx.html****  
**](https://www.nytimes.com/video/multimedia/100000002175079/audio-jimmie-foxx.html)**Born in 1907 in Sudlersville, on the eastern shore of Maryland, not far from the Delaware border. Grew up on a farm. Foxx was a dominant multi-sport high school athlete.**

  

**Home Run Baker, from nearby Trappe, MD, helped to found a Class D minor league team in Easton in 1924, and, as he was putting together a roster, Jimmie's parents showed up at his house and asked him to give their sixteen-year-old a tryout. It only took a couple of months for Baker to ring his old boss, Connie Mack, about Foxx, who was more than holding his own. He signed with Mack's Philadelphia A's for $2000 on July 30. His parents were understandably worried - things had moved faster than anyone could have predicted. Jimmie played out the season with Easton (and, due to a strange rule where playoff teams could pick up two players from teams that didn't make it, Parksley), then his uncle drove him to Philly and he rode the A's bench for the last two weeks of 1924.**

  

**And then he went back home, to start his senior year of high school. He was ineligible to play sports, but he reffed a few of Sudlerville High's soccer games. He didn't make it all the way to spring, though, after it was made clear to him that he'd be going to spring training with the A's. Philadelphia had been a dominant team from 1909-14, and was slowly building to another crest. Their rookie class of 1925 of Jimmie, Lefty Grove, and Mickey Cochrane is by far the greatest ever. (The dynasty's four star, Al Simmons was a sophomore.) Cochrane won the catcher's job out of camp over Cy Perkins, who had been the starting catcher for the previous six seasons. After a couple of early-season pinch-hitting appearances, Mack sent Foxx down to Providence for June and July, where, especially as Cochrane played well with the big club, Jimmie wound up playing more right field than anything else. (Went home in mid-June to attend his classmates’ high school graduation.) He was called up for September, by which point the Athletics were fading from the pennant race, but the A's were back, finishing second in the AL. Jimmie got nine at-bats and two innings behind the plate for the big club before his season ended early - he landed on his left shoulder when he tripped during a baserunning contest before an exhibition game.****

**\#29 Dwight Gooden**

**PHYSICAL DESCRIPTION: 6’2, 190. Even as a teenager he looked like an athlete, never super skinny.**

**FASTBALL: Big fastball, high 90s. In his elite early years, just threw the four-seamer. Developed a two-seamer later on, but the four-seam was the star.** 

**OFF-SPEED: Elite curveball, nicknamed “Lord Charles.” Pretty much just threw the A+ four-seam fastball and the A+ curveball at his peak, although he always had a changeup to mix in here and there. Picked up a slider later in his career.**

**CONTROL: Strong. Had good command of the fastball and curveball. Walks were never really a problem for him once he made the majors until after the drug suspension, though he did struggle with them in the minors.**

**DURABILITY: Between drugs and injuries, he was done as an all-time great pitcher by age 24 and as a solid major league starter by age 28.****

**\#32 Mike Trout**

**Seems to be a legitimately nice, happy guy. Signs autographs for kids before every game. "Perpetually upbeat." "Says he cannot ever remember being invited on to a late-night talk show." Very much "keep it simple", don't think too much type of guy. Works with charities.**

**Early in his career he struggled with the high fastball, then destroyed them.**

**Gave himself a rule in the minor leagues to take a strike every at-bat so that he'd see more pitches and improve his plate discipline.**

**Doesn't like to take BP on the field because he can't get enough work in, doesn't feel comfortable taking swings away from teammates.**

**Angels Stadium is a pitchers' park?**

**Probably the right pick if you're in the time machine / no steroids zone.**

**Lived in childhood bedroom in the offseason?**

**Not emotionless, Skaggs & brother-in-law**

**Wife Jessica Cox is high school sweetheart, proposed with skywriting:** [**https://twitter.com/JessTara/status/749367449150193665**](https://twitter.com/JessTara/status/749367449150193665)**, she's a teacher in New Jersey**

**2010 \#85 / 2011 \#2 / 2012 \#3"Mike has made decisions on what he wants to do, doesn't want to do, how he wants to spend his free time or not spend his free time," Manfred said in the hours before MLB's All-Star Game at Nationals Park. "I think we could help him make his brand very big" "But he has to make a decision to engage. It takes time and effort." "I have received lots of questions about Commissioner Manfred’s recent statement. I am not a petty guy and would really encourage everyone to just move forward. Everything is cool between the Commissioner and myself. End of story. I am ready to just play some baseball\!"**

**Podcast**

**Storm chasing** [**https://www.mlb.com/cut4/mike-trout-enjoys-arizona-snowstorm-c304280586**](https://www.mlb.com/cut4/mike-trout-enjoys-arizona-snowstorm-c304280586)**,** [**https://www.mlb.com/cut4/jim-cantore-sent-mike-trout-a-weather-balloon-c165371200**](https://www.mlb.com/cut4/jim-cantore-sent-mike-trout-a-weather-balloon-c165371200)

**PHYSICAL DESCRIPTION: 6'2, 235. One of the stronger guys around, even now.**

**HIT: Very good. Threat to win batting titles in his first two full seasons, found himself in the top 10 a few times since.**

**POWER: Excellent. A good power hitter early in his career, is debatably the best power hitter in the game at this point.**

**RUN: Excellent. Only six guys have ever had a season like he did in 2012, with 49 steals while only getting caught five times. (Max Carey, Tim Raines, Barry Larkin, Jacoby Ellsbury, Jerry Mumphrey.) Great athlete.**

**FIELD: Solid. His defensive numbers bounce up and down, highlights are pretty impressive. With his speed, though, it's hard to call him better than "solid" without great defensive numbers.**

**THROW: Good. Maybe a little above average for center field.**

**PLATE DISCIPLINE: Outstanding. Has walked 110 times in four seasons now. Was not a huge-walk guy in the minors or as a rookie, but developed quickly.**

**DURABILITY: Good. Missed a good chunk of 2017 with a thumb sprain, got September surgery on his foot in 2019 when the Angels were way out of the running.****

**\#36 Bob Gibson**

**PHYSICAL DESCRIPTION: Listed at 6'1, 189. Long arms. Three-quarters arm slot, big off-balance finish took him flying off to the first-base side on every pitch.**

**FASTBALL: Definitely threw smoke. Had a rising fastball and a sinking fastball. Certainly wasn't afraid to throw at hitters. There are a few guys in the 1960s who are all kind of bunched together for me at the top of the heap, velocity-wise - Koufax, Sam McDowell, Jim Maloney - and Gibson ranks right alongside them. (They're all behind Nolan Ryan, of course, but it feels wrong to call him a 1960s pitcher.)**

**OFF-SPEED: All-time great slider. His four-seam / slider combination doesn't even look real in black-and-white, I'm just not used to seeing pitchers from 50 years ago with stuff like that. Had a curveball and a changeup, as well.**

**CONTROL: Went from terrible in his first couple of years, to bad til about 1965, to pretty good from 1966-68, down to average for the rest of his career. Walked a ton of guys in his last two seasons.**

**DURABILITY: Outstanding. Outside of a broken leg in 1967, he essentially pitched full seasons until he was 38. He got a slow start due to his wildness - his first full season in the starting rotation wasn't until he was 25 - but it wasn't a durability issue.**

**\#38 Josh Gibson**

**  
****  
****PHYSICAL DESCRIPTION: He was a huge catcher for his time, 6'1 and 220. And he wasn't a fat guy, that 220 pounds is muscle. That's about 40 pounds heavier than Bill Dickey or Mickey Cochrane. Started to put on some bad weight in the 40s.**

**​HIT: Excellent. He's got a five-year stretch there where our available data lists him as hitting .364. Could really hit.**

**POWER: Elite. Certainly if you believe all the legends, he's the best ever. Evidence-wise, it's impossible to put him in the same category as Ruth, and hard to put him in the Foxx/McGwire/Bonds group, either. From the numbers on Baseball-Reference, he hit 67 homers in 1004 at-bats from 1934-43, which would put him at 37 per 550 at-bats. That's about Gehrig range, and it's not clear to me that we should expect power numbers to drop for Negro Leaguers around that time. So I think best-ever is a stretch, but I'm hesitant to claim there were more than five or ten better power hitters.**

**RUN: Solid, very good for a catcher. Not a guy who was stealing bases, but too many triples to be slow.**

**FIELD: Average. It's really hard to tell. As one of the most legendary figures of the Negro Leagues, it's not hard to find quotes of old players saying he was incredible defensively. I'm calling him average because the party line seems to be "he was really good" as opposed to "greatest defensive catcher you ever saw."**

**THROW: Average. Same thing as with fielding.**

**PLATE DISCIPLINE: Good. The Negro Leagues were not a high-walk environment, and he still drew some. On the other hand, his reputation did precede him, and he was pitched around accordingly.**

**​DURABILITY: Good. Didn't have many major injuries. His career wasn't as long as it could've been because he died young, but he was 35 and his weight and substance abuse issues were mounting by then - it's unlikely that he had many good seasons left.****

**\#40 Satchel Paige**

**PHYSICAL DESCRIPTION: Tall and lanky, 6'3, 180. After his arm injury, he started throwing sidearm and submarine occasionally. Took his time between pitches. I'm not entirely clear** **on what this means but Paige said of his pitching motion, "I use my single windup, my double windup, my triple windup, my hesitation windup, my no windup. I also use my step-n-pitch-it, my submariner, my sidearmer, and my bat dodger. Man's got to do what he's got to do."**

**VELOCITY: Elite. Definitely one of the hardest throwers around in the 1930s. Maintained good velocity, though not top-shelf velocity, into his 40s (at least). He definitely threw a four-seam fastball, which he called the jump ball, and another fastball called the be ball, which at the very least isn't too far from a two-seamer. Since you should always let Satchel explain things whenever possible: "I throw both overhand, only the be ball goes off my fingers on the smooth hide of the ball and rides on the level, and my jump ball I throw with my fingers across the seam and that makes it jump four to six inches." Another be ball quote:** **"My be ball is a be ball 'cause it be right were I want it, high and inside. It wiggles like a worm."** **He also threw a fastball he called the trouble ball. A surprising number of hitters talk about how Paige's fastball seemed tiny by the time it got up to the plate, "looked like a marble." Threw the hurry-up ball, the details of which I have not been able to hunt down, but if I know Satchel, I bet is what he called quick-pitching. Long Tom, Little Tom, and the midnight rider were three more fastballs. The "barber" was a brushback pitch at the chin, the "bow-tie" was a brushback pitch at the Adam's apple. It seems like the trouble ball was a fastball and the bat-dodger was not, but it's not entirely clear. And you gotta figure the smoke ball was some kind of fastball. There are people who claim he had a fastball called "Thoughtful Stuff," but I think that's just a misunderstanding stemming from this quote****: “I never joked when I was pitching. That ball I threw was thoughtful stuff.”** 

**OFF-SPEED: Pretty much threw nothing but fastballs until the mid-1930s. Developed a slow curve (claimed he had a single curve, a double curve, and a triple curve) and a changeup (which he called the nothin' ball). Invented the hesitation pitch in 1943: "The idea came to me in a game, when the guy at bat was all tighted up waiting for my fast ball. I knew he'd swing as soon as I just barely moved. So when I stretched, I paused just a little longer with my arms above my head. Then I threw my left foot forward but I didn't come around with my arm right away. I put that foot of mine down, stopping for a second, before the ball left my hand. When my foot hit the ground that boy started swinging, so by the time I came around with the whip he was way off stride and couldn't get anywhere near the ball." (He demonstrates it toward the end of the** ***I've Got a Secret*** **clip.) It was banned in MLB, through a rewrite of the impenetrable balk rule, after Paige's debut. By his mid-40s, Satchel was throwing some kind of screwball, some kind of slider, a blooper (which either he called the two-hump blooper or had, impossibly, two bloopers), some kind of knuckleball (whic****h he called the bat-dodger, and I suspect the wobbly ball was also some kind of knuckler), and a forkball he threw from sidearm and below that he called the whipsy-dipsy-do. I assume the four-day creeper was something slow, but you never know with Satchel. Paige: "I never threw an illegal pitch. Trouble is, once in awhile I would toss one that ain't never been seen by this generation." It should be noted in all this that his catchers didn't have different signals for all these - Slow Robinson said, "To this day, I still don't know which one Long Tom was." Descriptions of the nightmare pitch (which he "stayed up all night dreaming up"), slow gin fizz, alley-oop, and butterfly have been lost to history.**

**CONTROL: Maybe best ever. Particularly his pre-injury walk rates in the Negro Leagues are pretty excellent, although it's not like he's best in the league ever year. It is interesting, though, how many myths developed about Satchel's control - something that doesn't happen nearly as much with the other great control pitchers. That he would warm up throwing over a gum wrapper, for example. I'm not ruling out best-ever, that's all I'm saying.**

**DURABILITY: Maybe best ever. Had what appeared to be a career-ending injury in 1938, but was back by 1940 pitching full seasons. Threw a ton of innings, particularly before that. And in terms of longevity, well, he probably had the best non-knuckleballer career ever after age 43, including three shutout major-league innings at age 58. When he was a pitching coach with Atlanta in 1968, at age 62, there are quotes from players saying he still threw harder than some of their pitchers.****

**\#51 John McGraw**

**“The Little Napoleon.” In Cuba, “El Mono Amarillo” (e.g., The Yellow Monkey).**

**Would sometimes get thrown out of games early to go to the track.** 

**PHYSICAL DESCRIPTION: Little guy, 5'7" and 155. Looks pretty wide in most of the pictures you see of him, but those are typically from his managing days. Light in his playing days. Weighed 121 as a rookie.**

**HIT: Very good. Never came all that close to leading the league in batting average despite his speed. That said, hitting .342, .391, .344 from 1898-1900 is pretty darn good.**

**POWER: Below average. Never hit so many as 20 doubles in a season and had 13 homers for his career. When you're fast and you can hit, it's tough to wind up with fewer extra bases than that - below average may be overrating McGraw's power.**

**RUN: Excellent. Consistently ranked among the league leaders in steals at his peak.**

**FIELD: Solid. Wasn't a particularly good middle infielder early in his career, but was decent once he got moved to third full time in 1894.**

**THROW: Hard to know, but I suspect it's just OK - if you're fast and they try to move you from short to second, arm's usually the reason. On the other hand, they did move him back, and then to third, so it can't have been terrible.**

**PLATE DISCIPLINE: Possibly the best ever. Didn't draw walks quite at the rate of Bonds, Ruth, and Ted Williams, but is right there in the next tier, and those three guys all had months where they hit as many homers as John McGraw did in his whole career.**

**DURABILITY: Was the best player in baseball in 1900, but, when he started managing starting in 1899, he started limiting his own playing time - going from 650 plate appearances in his final pre-managing year of 1898 down to 447 in 1900, 223 in 1902, and 15 for his age-30 season of 1903. It's unclear how much of this is due to bad self-evaluation, how much is due to his body breaking down, and how much is due to him just wanting to focus on managing. Either way, he really never played a full season past the age of 26. Even before that, he missed big chunks of time in 1895 and, especially, 1896.**

[**https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=tpy4oS9-Ne0**](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=tpy4oS9-Ne0)

**John McGraw was born poor in Truxton, NY, just south of Syracuse, in 1873. His father was an Irish immigrant and a hard drinker who had already been widowed once, and, when John was 11, his mother and four of his siblings died in a diphtheria epidemic. A few months later, after a bad beating, John moved into the nearby Truxton House Inn, where a widow named Mary Goddard raised him alongside her two children.**

**A connection through the local Truxton baseball team got him a job with Olean in the New York-Penn League in 1890. After short pit stops in Cuba and Cedar Rapids, he was signed by Baltimore in August of 1891.** 

**McGraw and Robinson owned the Diamond Cafe in Baltimore.**

**For the 1900 season, he signed with St. Louis for $10,000 - the most in baseball, about $315,000 today - under the unprecedented condition that he, and his friend Wilbert Robinson, would not be subject to the reserve clause after the year.**

**\#64 Amos Rusie**

**PHYSICAL DESCRIPTION: Really big dude for 1890 at 6'1", 200 pounds. Three-quarters delivery. Right-handed.**

**FASTBALL: Probably the hardest thrower ever before 1900, though I wouldn't argue with someone who suggested it was Jouett Meekin. But Rusie was definitely the gold standard, typical point of comparison for fastball speed until Walter Johnson came along.**

**OFF-SPEED: Had a legitimately excellent curveball, noted for being one of the hardest-thrown breaking balls anyone had seen by that point. (I suspect we'd call it a slider today, but that's just a guess.) Mixed in a changeup.**

**CONTROL: Well below average. Rusie was up there to throw unhittable pitches - getting it over the plate was a nice side effect. Led the league in strikeouts five times and walks five times in a six-year span. Along with Bob Feller, Rusie is one of the only truly great pitchers ever to walk four batters per nine innings in his prime.**

**DURABILITY: Shoulder issues ended his career at age 27. Threw 500 innings in a season three times before that happened, though.**

**\#67 Cy Young**

**PHYSICAL DESCRIPTION: Huge for the 19th century, 6'2, 210. Righty, threw overhand but, later in his career, would mix in some sidearm as a different look for the hitter. (Even, every once in awhile, would go submarine.) Very little leg kick, closer to a slide step, but would partially turn his back to the hitter on the leg raise. Young said he had four different deliveries, but this seems like it was the main one.**

**FASTBALL: Really good in his youth. Didn't throw as hard as Amos Rusie in the 1890s, but wasn't far off - he and Jouett Meekin were right there battling for second. It faded some by the 1900s, and Young was forced to turn to his guile and off-speed stuff more.**

**OFF-SPEED: Pretty nonexistent while his fastball was top-notch. For most of the 1890s, he was working with a great fastball and great control and that was about it. As he aged, he added some junk. Breaking ball got pretty good. Had something that he called a "screwball or indrop" but we'd probably call a changeup today. Would mix in a spitball, as well.**

**CONTROL: Elite. Constantly led the league in fewest walks per nine innings. Definitely in the conversation for best ever.**

**DURABILITY: Ridiculous. All-time leader in innings, starts, complete games, batters faced - participated in about 4000 more batter-pitcher matchups than anyone else in major league history. (Pud Galvin is second.) Threw 300+ innings sixteen times, a mark nobody else approaches. Essentially had his first sore arm at age 43. Probably the most impressive of all-time, even when controlling for era. (Nolan Ryan, Don Sutton, Phil Niekro, Satchel Paige being the top contenders.)****  
**[**https://youtu.be/WWEpyXidZ4M?t=65****  
**](https://youtu.be/WWEpyXidZ4M?t=65)**Denton True Young was born in 1867 near Gilmore, Ohio, equidistant from Columbus, Pittsburgh, and Cleveland. He grew up on a farm and made it through the sixth grade in a two-room schoolhouse. "All us Youngs could throw. I usta kill squirrels with a stone when I was a kid and my granddad onct killed a turkey buzzard on the fly with a rock." He put together a local Gilmore team in the early 1880s and, with a few wins, started moving up the ladder of central Ohio semipro baseball. We don't know why, but, for the years of 1885-87, he and his father moved to Cowles, Nebraska, in the middle of the state, near the Kansas border. They worked on a farm there, Young formed a new team, and he later claimed he'd never lost a game in the state of Nebraska. Then they came home to Gilmore.**

  

**In 1890, at age 23, he got a real contract offer from a real minor league team in Canton, $60 a month. By late April, the nickname "Cyclone" had stuck hard enough that it was appearing in box scores. In July, the Cleveland Spiders bought his contract from Canton for $300.**

  

**A few years later, he married Robba Miller, who grew up on the farm next door.****
