**\#77 Tommy Bond**

**PHYSICAL DESCRIPTION: Little guy at 5'7", 160 pounds, below average even in a time when nobody on his team was six feet tall or 200 pounds. Threw underhand, according to the rules of the time, but liked to (by the book, illegally) creep up toward sidearm.**

**VELOCITY: Very good for the time.**

**CONTROL: Excellent. Typically among the league leaders in fewest walks per nine innings. As an 18-year-old rookie in 1874, he walked eight hitters in 497 innings, something nobody else has even come close to doing. (I'm sure Dolly Gray, when he walked eight guys in one inning in 1909, would've appreciated the nine balls to a walk that Tommy got.)**

**OFF-SPEED: Threw a very effective curveball, one of the earlier pitchers to rely on it. Sometimes also threw a "sponge ball," which involved him throwing what was essentially a spitball, but using a wet sponge instead of saliva to moisten his fingers.**

**DURABILITY: On the one hand, he threw the great majority of the innings his teams played for seven years running. On the other, most pitchers were also doing that and Tommy was done at age 24, mostly due to arm issues.**

**\#86 Ross Barnes**

**PHYSICAL DESCRIPTION: Smaller side even for 1871 at 5'8" and 145 pounds.**

**HIT: Outstanding. Hit .430 in 1872, .431 in 1873, and .429 in 1876, three of the five highest season batting averages ever. (The other two being Tip O'Neill hitting .435 in 1887 and Hugh Duffy hitting .440 in 1894.) Obviously a little different than hitting .430 for the rest of baseball history, but he was dominant. Great at taking advantage of a rule that didn't outlast him by much, where a bunt that landed fair but then rolled past the foul line was considered a fair ball.**

**POWER: Solid. Only hit six home runs in his career, but whole teams in his career only averaged about four homers a year. Led the league in doubles and triples a couple times each.**

**RUN: Very good. Went right in the tank when his illness in 1877 started up.**

**FIELD: Excellent. Mostly a second baseman, played a little short and a little third. Has a good argument as the most impactful fielder of the NA era.**

**THROW: No idea.**

**PLATE DISCIPLINE: Very good. Not a lot of walks in the nine-balls-to-a-walk era of the NA, but he had the second-highest walk rate in the league after Denny Mack from 1871-76.**

**DURABILITY: Major issues. Essentially done as a player by age 26.**

**SP \#53: Carl Hubbell**

  

**PHYSICAL DESCRIPTION: Six feet, 170 pounds. Left handed, had a fairly lazy, whippy arm motion.**

**FASTBALL: Solid but not spectacular.**

**OFF-SPEED: Outstanding. Out pitch was his screwball, which was probably the greatest ever. Had a big, sharp-breaking screwball and would play a fading changeup/screwball mix off it. Threw a real curveball, too. Seems a lot of his success came from being able to use those three off-speed angles that are very rare to find in one pitcher.**

**CONTROL: Excellent, consistently among the league leaders in fewest walks per nine innings. Really knew how to pitch.**

**DURABILITY: Always healthy until about age 35, then battled injuries to the end.**

[**https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=93qb2SqR-DY**](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=93qb2SqR-DY)

  

**Carl Hubbell grew up on a farm in rural Oklahoma. He made good on a tryout and started pitching in the Class D Oklahoma State League at 20. He moved up from there, but missed nearly the entire 1924 season, at age 21, with a case of typhoid fever. When he came back, he started experimenting with the screwball, and Detroit bought his rights after the 1925 season.**

  

**In spring training with the Tigers, catcher Johnny Bassler called Hubbell’s new off-speed “the screwiest pitch I’ve ever seen,” and Hubbell’s eventual success made “screwball” the popular name for what was previously more often called the fadeaway. Detroit management quickly had him drop the pitch, regardless, because they worried it would be too hard on his arm. He still convinced his minor league managers to let him throw it here and there, but it was mostly tabled for a few years.**

  

**By 1928, Hubbell was 25, and when Detroit sent him to the minors for the third straight season, it meant they no longer controlled his rights. Hubbell took the demotion as license to resurrect the screwball, and his contract was bought by the New York Giants in July for $50,000, a number that shows they thought he was a real prospect.**

**SP \#114: Al Spalding**

**PHYSICAL DESCRIPTION: Big guy for the era at 6'1, 170. Threw underhand, as everyone did at the time, and seemed to have a somewhat funky windup, though how it was funky isn't entirely clear to me. Liked to mess with hitters' timing by quick-pitching or holding the ball a long time before throwing.**

**VELOCITY: Threw pretty hard, but his pitching brain is credited for his success more than raw speed.**

**OFF-SPEED: Had a changeup.**

**CONTROL: Above average, not elite, judging by walk numbers. But it seems to me it must've been quite good generally, because he wasn't the hardest thrower in baseball and he was mostly working with a fastball and a changeup. (Walks were so rare in those days, taking eight balls as they did, that it's hard to take the walk numbers as gospel; in 1874, Spalding walked 19 guys in 617 innings and didn't lead the league in fewest walks per nine.)**

  

**HIT: Above average. Hit .313 for his career and finished in the top 10 in 1872. No strikeouts in 365 plate appearances in 1874.**

**POWER: Above average. Only two career homers but hit a fair number of doubles.**

**RUN: Solid.**

**FIELD: When he was primarily a pitcher, he spent almost all his non-pitching time in centerfield. But in 1877, he was primarily a first baseman and played a fair amount of second as well. Seems like he was a pretty good first baseman, not least of all because he wore a glove and a bunch of other idiot first basemen still did not.**

**DURABILITY: Pitched nearly every inning his teams played before hurting his arm in 1877. Was essentially done as a player at age 27, but it appears that was mostly seen as a "promotion to management" as opposed to being done as a player. (It's unclear whether the injury would've prevented him from pitching again beyond 1877.)**

**SS \#13: Jack Glasscock**

**PHYSICAL DESCRIPTION: He was 5'8", 160, which was about average for a major leaguer in the 1880s.**

**HIT: For most of his career, just OK, but won the 1890 batting title and finished second in 1889.**

**HIT FOR POWER: Limited, even for his time. Averaged a couple homers a year. Decent doubles hitter, particularly in his 1889-90 hitting peak.**

**RUN: Very good athlete. They didn't keep steals numbers until he was 28 and he still stole 50 bases three times.**

**FIELD: One of, if not the, most impactful defensive players of the 19th century - possibly the all-time greatest gloveless fielder. Definitely dropped off as a fielder after an 1891 hand injury. And because fielding was harder, there was more differentiation between fielders. For example, Glasscock committed 40 errors in 1883, but Boston Beaneaters shortstop Sam Wise committed 88 in 96 games. No shorstop in the 2019 major leagues committed more than 26. Great fielders were more important.**

**THROW: Seems to have a fine arm. Hard to know for sure, but tough to be a great shortstop with no arm.**

**PLATE DISCIPLINE: Mediocre. Offensively, relied on putting the ball in play and being fast.**

**DURABILITY: Excellent. Played in most of his teams' games every year from 1879-93.**

**1879 (Cleveland Blues 2B): really struggled as a starter**

**SS \#19: George Wright**

**Had a real impact on the introduction of both golf and tennis to America, in addition to baseball. The first tennis clubs started popping up in the 1880s, and, though mostly due to Ditson, Wright & Ditson became one of the first tennis racket importers and the manufacturers of the official tennis ball. His son, Beals, won the tennis singles’ gold medal at the 1904 St. Louis Olympics and was one of the earlier inductees into the tennis Hall of Fame. (And his other son, Irving, won a couple of U.S. Open mixed doubles championships in the 1910s.)**

  

**In 1896, he opened the second public golf course in America, nine holes at Franklin Park in Boston. Francis Ouimet, the 20-year-old amateur golfer who won the 1913 U.S. Open, was working at Wright’s sporting goods store when he entered the tournament.**

  

**He coached tennis, ice hockey, and baseball later in life - including bringing a team to the 1912 Stockholm Olympics for exhibitions. Went on the 1888-89 Spalding World Tour as an umpire.**

  

**Wright & Ditson sporting goods was a major manufacturer for quite a while. George bought the patent for the catcher’s mask in 1875.**

  

**PHYSICAL DESCRIPTION: Stood 5’9” and weighed 150, which wasn’t quite as small as it sounds in 1873.**

**HIT: Good. Certainly an above average hitter, but never really approached a batting title, not least because he always trailed Ross Barnes by a fair margin.**

**POWER: Very good. He played in a time where extra base power was much more tightly correlated with speed than it is now - a lot more legging out triples, a lot less watching home runs fly over the fence - but he consistently ranked with the jets like Barnes and Lip Pike in isolated power.**

**RUN: Very good. Stole his share of bases and led the league in triples once.**

**FIELD: Excellent. One of the better barehanded shortstops around, and definitely in the conversation for most impactful defender of the 1870s.**

**THROW: Really good. Popularized playing shortstop behind the baseline - one of the earliest guys who had a good enough arm to pull it off. Also worth noting that, in the barehand era, he would throw with either hand. Hard to believe he was throwing left-handed rockets from behind second base or anything, but he does appear to have made some lefty throws in games.**

**PLATE DISCIPLINE: Above average. That feels strange to say about a guy who never walked more than 13 times in a season, but it took nine balls to walk then and the seasons were shorter. (In 1874, someone named Candy Nelson led the NA with nine walks on the year.)**

**DURABILITY: Pretty good, though he's a trickier case than most. He definitely was injured for part of the 1871 season, but it seems like he was otherwise fairly healthy, even reaching back into the late 1860s. On the other hand, you wonder if something happened as part of his 1880-81 leave from the game, because he was just such a disaster in his 1882 return. But it was only 46 games, and he did play competitive cricket for a surprisingly long time after that for a 35-year-old, so who knows.**

**I didn't do rankings for pre-1870 seasons, because I'd be guessing to the extreme, but there's a real argument for placing him at the top in 1869 and 1870.**

  

**George Wright was born in Harlem in 1847, one of five boys. His parents were English, and his oldest brother, Harry, was a year old when the family crossed the Atlantic in 1836. His father, Samuel, was a serious cricket player - it’s my understanding that his full-time job was running the St. George’s Cricket Club in New York - and all five boys were playing early on. Harry and George took to it the most, and Harry was well on his way to becoming a cricket pro himself, and a baseball player on the side, by the time George was a teenager.**

  

**A hair too young for Civil War duty, George played a little catcher and outfield for the Gotham club in New York in 1863, at age 16, and then moved to Philadelphia in 1864 to become club pro at the Philadelphia Cricket Club. He played for one of the city’s top two baseball clubs, too, the Olympic, for the next two years.**

**  
****Paying players wasn’t legal in baseball in the 1860s, but the Union of Morrisania club, back in New York City, found a little under-the-table money for him and George started playing shortstop for them in 1866. The following year, the Washington Nationals found a bunch of clerking jobs (which seemed to require very limited attendance) for a series of good ballplayers, one of whom was George. The Nationals went on a tour that took them as far as Illinois and acquitted themselves well, blasting Harry’s Cincinnati club by a score of 53-10. The Unions brought George back for 1868, and George started trying to get into the sporting goods business on the side.**

  

**Then the National Association of Base Ball Players ruled that professionalism was legal, and Harry got serious about putting together the best team in America. George, 24 by this time, was probably the best baseball player in the world, and received the highest contract on his older brother’s all-professional 1869 Cincinnati Red Stockings, in the range of about $1500 for the year (about $30,000 today). In Cincinnati, he moved in with Harry, Harry’s recently-married second wife, Carrie, and Harry’s two sons from his first marriage.**

  

**Cincinnati won all 67 of their games, becoming the first team to tour both coasts and, by far, the most famous baseball team to date. George hit .629.**

  

**The Red Stockings rolled the same nine guys out there for 1870 and finally got beaten on June 14, by a solid Brooklyn Atlantics team featuring Lip Pike, George Zettlein, and Dickey Pearce. Cincinnati was in the hunt but Chicago took the 1870 NABBP official championship. The club directors reacted to this, and rising salary demands, by disbanding the Red Stockings.**

  

**Harry founded the Boston Red Stockings over the winter, as an original member of the new National Association. He brought George and Cal McVey from Cincinnati, and signed two elite young players from Rockford, Ross Barnes and Al Spalding. They were, comfortably, the most talented team in the NA, even considering that George missed half the short season with a leg injury after a collision, but finished just short of Philadelphia for the 1871 championship.**

  

**Over the next four years, Boston was dominant. Ross Barnes was the best player in baseball, George was second, and Deacon White, Al Spalding, and Cal McVey were all in the conversation for third. The Red Stockings went 39-8 to win the 1872 title, 43-16 to win the 1873 title, 52-18 to win the 1874 title, and a near-impossible 71-8 to win the 1875 title.**

  

**Before that dominant season was entirely over, Chicago owner William Hulbert recruited Barnes, White, Spalding, and McVey to his squad for 1876. (Since Harry ran the show in Boston, George wasn’t going anywhere.) The NA didn’t appreciate Hulbert’s under-the-table dealings, so Hulbert just went and formed the National League instead of facing the punishment. Chicago rolled to the championship - George and Jim O’Rourke couldn’t do it alone.**

  

**By 1877, George was 30, and his peak was ending. He found himself playing almost exclusively second base and, while still a strong fielder, had a below-average hitting season. Boston made two huge signings over the winter - Deacon White came back for another stint, and 21-year-old Irishman Tommy Bond, who had established himself as the best pitcher in the world, was swiped from Hartford. Boston cruised to the title.**

  

**The next year, White left again and George had an awful season, hitting a fairly empty .225. Bond, without much help, brought Boston another championship anyway.**

  

**After eight years in Boston and ten straight playing with Harry, George played the 1879 season a few miles south for the Providence Grays. He brought Jim O’Rourke with him, joining Paul Hines, one of the better outfielders in baseball, and a two-man pitching staff: young hotshot John Ward and a slightly past-his-prime Bobby Mathews. George put together his last good season and the Grays pulled out the championship over Boston.**

  

**In the first nine years of organized baseball league play, George Wright was on the best team seven times, and that on the heels of two monster Cincinnati seasons. When the best player in baseball is the brother of the best team architect in baseball, well, that works pretty well.**

  

**George decided to head back to Boston for 1880, but his plans were stymied by the leaguewide addition of the reserve clause, which did quite a bit to limit the kind of team switching that was previously commonplace. He turned down Providence’s best offer and decided to focus on his sporting goods business instead of playing. Somehow, he made it onto the field for Boston for one game in May, I assume because it was still the wild west and people would just do things like that. (I suspect the boys in Providence didn’t appreciate it.)**

  
  
  

**He married Abbie Coleman in 1873 and had two sons and two daughters with her. She died in 1913.**

  

**Note: If I had rankings for 1868, 1869, and 1870, George would probably be \#1 on all three, but those rankings wouldn’t be based on much.​**

**LF \#21: Jesse Burkett**

**PHYSICAL DESCRIPTION: Left-handed little guy at 5'8", 155.**

**HIT: Excellent. Great bat control, known for his ability to bunt and to foul off pitches. Won three NL batting titles.**

**HIT FOR POWER: Fine. Nobody was really hitting homers in the 1890s, but neither was he. Best showing was 10 in 1901, which ranked third in the league. Hit some doubles and triples, but not special gap power, either.**

**RUN: Good speed in Cleveland, 30 steals a year type. Went down to just solid as he aged.**

**FIELD: Generally average. Struggled early in his career. Led the league in errors a few times.**

**THROW: Weak arm.**

**PLATE DISCIPLINE: Solid. Tended to get 70 walks or so a year.**

**DURABILITY: Excellent, rarely missed games until his retirement at age 36.**

**RF \#10: Elmer Flick**

**PHYSICAL DESCRIPTION: 5'9", 168. Left-handed hitter, choked way up on the bat.**

**HIT: Excellent. Consistently found himself among the batting average leaders.**

**HIT FOR POWER: Decent. Peaked at 11 homers in 1900, but that wasn't nothing at the time and he was a real doubles and triples threat.**

**RUN: Very good. Led the league in triples and steals multiple times. Natural athlete who played football, boxed, and wrestled successfully in his youth.**

**FIELD: Nothing out of the ordinary, numbers-wise. Considering that he was fast and playing right field, though, you'd expect them to be a little better.**

**THROW: Seems like it was pretty good, but hard to know. Threw out a decent number of baserunners, at least.**

**PLATE DISCIPLINE: Not bad. Around 60 walks a year in a low walk era.**

**DURABILITY: Rarely missed games until the illness struck in 1908.**

**FIELD: SS Rabbit Maranville**

**PHYSICAL DESCRIPTION: Only 5'5" and 155 pounds. Right-handed.**

**HIT: Average, at his best. Around age 30, went into below average territory.**

**POWER: Average at his best, below average in his thirties. Got more doubles and triples than you would think for a tiny guy who doesn't hit great.**

**RUN: Very good. Got to a lot of balls and stole 20 bases a year. Started to dip around when he left Pittsburgh.**

**DEFENSE: Outstanding. Remained surprisingly good deep into his thirties.**

**PLATE DISCIPLINE: Good. Drew about 50 walks a year despite being a bad hitter.**

**DURABILITY: Great. Rarely hurt, played forever - one of the few players who passed his expiration date before running into injury issues.**

**MYSTERY: RF Chino Smith**

**PHYSICAL DESCRIPTION: Stood about 5'10, looks like he weighed 175 or so. Usually listed as smaller than that.**

**HIT: Outstanding. Hit in the range of .400 lifetime - obviously he wouldn't have done that in the big leagues, but, equally obviously, that's ridiculous and shouldn't be ignored. There aren't guys who floated around the minors, put up numbers like that, and weren't good major leaguers.**

**HIT FOR POWER: Solid. Probably a 20 homers a year type in the majors. His numbers look more impressive than that, but he played his two big power seasons in a park that was 180 down the lines and 292 to center.**

**RUN: Good. Could steal a base, but not a jet.**

**FIELD: Seems to have been fine. It can be hard to tell with the more obscure Negro League players - you can find a quote for seemingly anyone suggesting that they were excellent in the field. Did start his career at second base, which suggests he had at least some range and hands.**

**THROW: Solid, nothing special for right field.**

**PLATE DISCIPLINE: Didn't walk much early on, but they started coming at the same time as the rest of his offense. Probably 80-100 walks a year in the majors.**

**DURABILITY: Had a pretty major collision injury and died young of illness.**

**RANKINGS EXPLAINED, \#1-2**

The conventional wisdom is that Babe Ruth is the greatest baseball player of all-time. To get from there to ranking Pedro \#1, you essentially have to flip three core assumptions:

  

1.  Valuation of starting pitchers.
2.  Era.
3.  Peak vs. longevity.

  

Let’s take these one at a time.

  

VALUATION OF STARTING PITCHERS  
If you have one game to play, your \#1 pick should be the best starting pitcher. It’s not remotely close. The gap in value of 25-30 hitters’ worth of Pedro versus 4-6 plate appearances of Ruth is astronomical. The conventional wisdom has been, essentially, to value starting pitchers based on their regular season rest schedule, with confusion reigning with respect to era-based rest differences. I’m not going so far as to put everyone in a one-game scenario - that’d give us a Hall of Fame of 90% pitchers - but I don’t think it’s unreasonable to treat starters like they’re all pitching every fourth game, which every ace seems capable of doing come playoff time.

  

ERA

First of all, it's not *really* even an option to take the best-out-of-the-time-machine approach. For one, it's awfully boring to have 75 of your top 100 come from the last 25 years. For another, it either shrinks or erases a massive and entertaining portion of baseball history. And, even if the ability of the players is increasing more slowly every year, it's incredibly annoying to think about someone in 2060 spitting on the talent of all our current stars. So what we have to use as our measuring stick is a player's impact in their time. (How much they "moved the needle," you see.)

  

That said, I'm still going to scoot guys around some based on how much baseball was really baseball when they played. This comes into play most when we get pre-1900 - if you think it's bad trying to convince yourself Ruth was better than Bonds, try convincing yourself Ross Barnes was in the same stratosphere - but it's not even close to gone by Ruth's time.

  

Balls that bounced over the fence were called home runs in the American League in all four seasons Ruth hit 50, and there was even a bizarre transition year (1930) where they were homers in the NL and doubles in the AL. Nobody threw sliders, but there were still guys throwing spitballs. There weren't lefty one-out relievers, but they were playing multiple games a day and going on weeks-long road trips without planes. They'd use the same ball for innings at a time, and it's hard enough to see a new baseball when it's dusk and you have no lights and you're racing to end the game before the umpire decides it's time.

  

There's the issue of race, and the breadth of the reach of MLB. Obviously, for all of Ruth's career, black players were not signed by Major League teams, but it's also true that only a very few Hispanic players did. Baseball in Asia was just getting started. Scouting networks were developing, certainly more than I expected to find when I started this project, but the fact still remains that St. Louis was as far south and as far west as MLB reached (and only ten cities were represented at all). Even if they were finding the good players in Texas, interest just wasn't at the same level there as it is now. And as far as interest? The pre-WWII attendance record was about 17,000 per game for the 1920 Yankees. The flip side of all this is expansion, when you're comparing a player to the league. You'd think that the talent distribution has to be wider in the early years, and that's true, but the nice thing is that expansion keeps a decent check on that gap - by the time baseball's fully integrated, and dramatically increasing its talent pool, teams start getting added, and that keeps happening until the last 20 years, when the pool has largely been stable. (In other words, even though Ruth is 10 wins above average in 1921, that'd put him 10 wins above the, to oversimplify, \#24 outfielder in the league, while Bonds in 2002 is 10 wins above average, or the \#45 outfielder in the now-larger league...which would be a trickier problem if the talent pool expansion didn't largely keep the same pace.)

  

Then there's money. There was hysteria when Ruth's 1930 salary was $80,000, worth about $1.2 million today, and he was the best player in baseball by 10 miles. In his first full season with Boston in 1915, he made $2,500 for the year, about $62,000 today. Baseball players were known drinkers, carousers, and gamblers, and if things went well and you made the major leagues, you could make, you know, a decent salary. College vs. baseball was a legitimate financial decision, even for a pretty good player. Plus it was just kind of a grimy, disreputable crew you'd be hanging around with. Now, every kid with a chance to play in the majors in 25 countries gets fully encouraged and pushed to do so, and, maybe more important, just about every kid tries enough to figure out if they have a chance.

  

So there's that issue of the player population pool we're dealing with, and then there are the game-wide improvements: advancement in injury prevention, weight training, mechanics... and those are really the things that stand out when you watch those clips, the clear strength and swing fluidity advantages of Bonds.

  

I’m planning to *largely* treat every championship as equally important, with a recognizability function along these lines:

  

So it’s clear that the 1870s aren’t remotely the same, but by 1900 we’re very close to taking everything as seriously as we would today.

  
  

PEAK

  

This is my biggest quibble with the way players are currently valued by the sabermetric community. I think the point that I realized something stupid was happening was when Rafael Palmeiro retired.

  

Palmeiro retired with 3000 hits and 500 homers, which meant that, if it wasn’t for steroid suspicions, he would’ve unquestionably elected to the Hall of Fame. What alarmed me was that it wasn’t just baseball fan media members who were on his side, but also the stats people. Baseball-Reference has him with 72 WAR, which is well above the 50 that is generally seen as the Hall of Fame line. But for the first time, really, I had been paying attention for basically Palmeiro’s entire career, and I had never for one second cared about trying to watch him play. He’d been like the 20th best player in baseball for awhile and then stuck around forever.

  

Then Johan Santana came onto the ballot, got 2% of the vote and disappeared. Santana had a legitimate stretch as the best pitcher in baseball, and it wasn’t remotely close for a good chunk of it. His changeup was a magic trick. This wasn’t a stats vs. scouts thing - the stats said that Santana was the best pitcher in baseball and Palmeiro wasn’t close to being the best hitter - we were just adding everything up funky at the end.

  

Essentially, we’ve gotten to the point where 10 wins’ worth of value in one season is seen as basically as important to the “greatness” discussion as 10 wins’ worth of value, spread across ten seasons. The mainstream statistical community seems to think that the most important indicator of greatness is playing time.

  

For example, there’s always lots of talk of career hits, career home runs, etc., when discussing the Hall of Fame. But the truth is that you get to about \#34 on the career home run list before you reach someone who’s not firmly in the Hall of Fame discussion (Carlos Delgado), but it takes until \#38 on the career at-bats list (Johnny Damon). The longer you’re allowed to stay in a major-league lineup, regardless of your ability, the more opportunity you get to do the two most helpful things toward making the Hall of Fame: rack up career statistics, and play in the World Series.

  

This second route is usually the saving grace for the short-career guys who best prove my point. There’s nobody better for this than Sandy Koufax. Koufax essentially had four years in his career where he was a full-time starting pitcher, and he was the best pitcher in baseball, by a fairly wide margin at times, for the five years from 1962-66. It is not hard to find people, non-statistical people usually, who think he’s the best pitcher ever. 

  

Despite the shortness of his career, his short-term dominance is recognized as extraordinary enough to be discussed alongside the all-time innings leaders. In my opinion, this is because his World Series dominance, on top of his overall dominance, made it unusually clear that he was something different.

  

Let’s look at how a few standard ways of viewing players historically see him:

  - By Baseball-Reference career pitching WAR, he ranks 89th.
  - By JAWS - the most commonly-cited Hall of Fame monitoring statistic in use today - he ranks 90th among starting pitchers. (JAWS combines a player’s career WAR with his 7-year peak WAR number.)
  - Tom Tango suggests that rather than using Wins Above Replacement (WAR), we should add up a player’s positive season Wins Above Average, essentially because doing that erases the value of being mediocre. It’s a start. Koufax ranks 70th among pitchers. Even then, we’re doing things like claiming Ted Lyons is better, essentially because he threw 2000 more innings of slightly above average baseball.

  

I think the biggest reason career stats are so popular is because anything better is messy. If you want to focus in on a player’s peak, do you look at one season? Three? Five? If one player’s much better than another for four seasons, but much worse in the fifth, doesn’t that make the choice between three- and five-year ranges look silly? If we just want to know how good the player is at his best, doesn’t it have to just be comparing their best season?

  

If you do this, though, it comes back with some answers that are hard to stand behind. First of all, if you go by Baseball-Reference WAR, Pud Galvin’s 1884 season is the greatest ever, coming in at 20.5 WAR. He threw 636 innings that year, which is basically three Pedro seasons.

  

OK, how about we look at WAR per game pitched in a season? Well, first, that leads us to George Stultz, who threw a complete game and allowed two unearned runs in his only start of the offense-heavy 1894 season (and, for that matter, the only start of his career). That’s silly, let’s try that for 20+ games pitched, listing just the top season for any one pitcher:

1.  Pedro Martinez, 2000 (0.40)
2.  Roger Clemens, 1997 (0.35)
3.  Dwight Gooden, 1985 (0.35)
4.  Greg Maddux, 1995 (0.35)
5.  Bob Gibson, 1968 (0.33)
6.  Jim McCormick, 1884 (0.32)
7.  Lefty Grove, 1936 (0.32)
8.  Russ Ford, 1910 (0.32)
9.  Zack Greinke, 2009 (0.32)
10. Walter Johnson, 1913 (0.31)
11. Mark Fidrych, 1976 (0.31)
12. Aaron Nola, 2018 (0.31)

  

I’m stopping with Fidrych and Nola because I think they’re illustrative of the problems with something as simple as one-year WAR per game. 

  
  
  

  - Peak stats are harder
  - Focus on wins, replacement -\> average -\> number of teams
  - Longevity / fame / postseason

  
  
  

My plan is, basically, to be for whoever blew the most minds in their time. (Although I’m certainly not above saying a player should have blown more minds, if only the fans were paying closer attention.) If you’re giving me one 

  
  

1\. SP Pedro Martinez (2000, BOS)

He was just so unhittable at his peak. I think him vs. Johnson is a fair fight, but it's tough to put together a good argument that any other pitcher was better than him at his best, in 1999 and 2000 - you pretty much either have to ignore league difficulty or put a huge premium on throwing a ton of innings. As far as the question of how high the highest pitcher should rank, my only stance is "this seems fine".

  

2\. RF Babe Ruth (1923, NYY):

I feel like the only explanation that really is necessary here is a link to my discussion of how I'm treating era. But yeah, I’ll take time machine 2002 Bonds first if we’re taking guys that way.

  

17\. SP Walter Johnson (1913, WAS)

Pedro was only the best pitcher in baseball for like three seasons, and Walter was for seven or eight. But at their best, Pedro was only giving up slightly more runs even though he was pitching in the steroid era. I broke the tie this way not least of all because it just seems ridiculous to say the best pitcher ever basically was just throwing fastballs, but if you lean into the innings numbers it's not hard to flip them.

  

6\. LF Ted Williams (1946, BOS)

​Was either at war or the best hitter in the majors for a decade and a half. I'm definitely open to the argument that he should be slightly higher - he missed his age 24, 25, and 26 seasons in World War II, and was at the absolute peak of his game immediately before or after. Another guy whose peak is more impressive than his career totals, though for different reasons than Mantle's.

  

7\. LF Barry Bonds (2004, SFG)

​Similarly, I don't think this is controversial outside of my stance on steroids.

  

8\. SS Honus Wagner (1908, PIT)

I may be a hair lower on Wagner than most, but I suspect this is uncontroversial.

  

10\. CF Willie Mays (1965, SFG)

I think he's gotten a lot of extra credit for (1) playing forever and racking up home runs, (2) being a five-tool player, and (3) lack of viable candidates from the 70s, 80s, and 90s to put above the old guys. (And the 00s, too, for the steroid haters). His peak was long, but not as high as some of these other guys. The biggest problem for me is that he was a very good hitter, one of the best of the 60s, but everyone else in the top 10 was an unbelievable hitter.

  

11\. CF Mickey Mantle (1957, NYY)

Highest season OBP since 1900 for a guy who was giving you anything in the field. Mays was an all-time great centerfielder, but Mantle had four or five seasons where he was a better hitter than any year Mays ever had. He suffers in career totals comparisons because he broke down in his early 30s, and I think he's been unfairly downgraded historically because of that.

  

12\. 2B Rogers Hornsby (1924, STL)

Flatly underrated. Most problematic for him was the constant comparisons with Ruth. First of all, it's tough for him, historically, because he's just worse than Ruth pretty much every year. Second, Ruth won seven titles, helped by Gehrig and Tris Speaker, while Hornsby only got the one. Third, Hornsby didn't have anything to hang his hat on quite like Ruth's home runs - he wasn't the average hitter Cobb was, so he winds up not being the greatest ever at anything. Fourth, he was a jerk. And fifth, his second base defense is sometimes so demeaned that it can feel like you ought to just consider him an average first baseman, which is just wrong. I think the argument's better to push him up than down.

  

The overarching goal here is this: To essentially host a complete history of baseball, without getting boring. It seems to me that, typically, you can find a contained, well-told story, or an intricately-detailed slog. My theory of the case is, basically, that there’s a fun version of Wikipedia pages.

  

​My hope is that both my girlfriend and my dad - neither of whom know Michel Enriquez and both of whom know Barry Bonds, but it’s very obvious which one will know Frankie Frisch - can both read the site, without getting bored for opposite reasons.

  

​The seed of this site grew from my most-recent attempt to rank the greatest players in baseball history. (As we all must from time to time.) So that’s where the plan began - with articles for everyone above the Hall of Fame line.

  

I should, at least briefly, define that line. First, I’m happy to induct players mid-career - it’s not real, so if I decide later to kick them out, it’s fine. Second, I certainly don’t care if someone was banned from baseball. (I believe I’ve inducted three such players, plus quite a few of the unofficial steroid bans, and I considered inducting two more.) Third, I didn’t restrict myself to Major League Baseball, or, as the real Hall does, to America. So we’re a little bigger than the real Hall.

  

The other thing that I realized quickly while putting this all together was this: A few of the most electrifying players in baseball history were really only solid, in terms of winning games. Frequently, I found that these were guys with one elite skill and a couple of major drawbacks. My solution was the specialty wing: I added a couple of players for each of the five tools (hit, hit for power, run, field, and throw). I also included, in the specialty wing, some mystery players - guys who I don’t feel confident are deserving of a place on the merits, but who have a decent argument for being comfortably deserving. (In almost all cases, this is a lack-of-information problem.)

  

Player evaluation - system description, peak (?)

Rankings - say what they all are, explain they’re vague

cities and leagues

  

\#32 Mike Trout

I've got him as the best player in baseball for eight consecutive years now (2012-2019), and it really hasn't been particularly close in any of them. That makes him one of three that I've had as \#1 eight times: Ruth, Williams, Trout. He may still have a season in him that throws him into the stratosphere.

  

\#35 Bullet Rogan

​While I do think it's fairly easy, typically, to translate baseball stats from one league to another, the Negro Leagues do present a couple of unique difficulties.

  

When you're trying to do a simpler translation, say, from AAA to the major leagues, you can base it on lots of players, moving in both directions, for many years in a row. But, of course, since the basis of the Negro Leagues' existence is segregation, you really wind up with all the best translation you can do being based on a few players, largely young, all going in the same direction, over the span of 1947-50 or so, while their very movement is deteriorating the league they leave behind (along with a bunch of guys who are stuck in the newly-integrated minors for a year or two post-Negro League ball). All of which is a little annoying from an analytic perspective, but is drastically exacerbated by the fact that these years are coming on the heels of World War II, when both leagues being considered are weakened, and the reliability / availability of stats coming out of the Negro Leagues falls off a cliff. So you're comparing 63 Jackie Robinson plate appearances for the Kansas City Monarchs in a war-troubled 1945 league to a full season for the Dodgers in 1947, which is only so useful.

  

This is dwarfed by the real problem, though, which is that a lot of the people who have attempted these translations then go back and act like it would have stayed the same for the 50 years previous. Which they have to do, if you're being mathematical about it, because here's close to a full list of guys who play in the Negro Leagues and majors between Fleet Walker in 1884 and Jackie Robinson: Dolf Luque, Armando Marsans, Rafael Almeida, Mike Gonzalez. The Negro Leagues data is best between about 1920 and 1940, and we see almost nobody go to the majors during this time frame.

  

But the Negro Leagues don't track identically with the majors. It's clear that black Americans, for obvious reasons, were coming to baseball later than white Americans. The clearest proof we have of this is that, through 1889, black players were playing in the minor leagues, and Frank Grant is really the only player during that time period who shines through as one of the best players in baseball. (As opposed to the breaking of the color line, where we immediately see Jackie, Larry Doby, Roy Campanella, and Willie Mays rise to the top.) And it doesn't help that, for big chunks of great careers - particularly before the 1920 creation of the Negro National League, but elsewhere, too - the stats we're getting are both spotty and coming against wildly varying levels of competition.

  

I say all this like I've really done the work here, which I most certainly have not. My favorite translations come from the Hall of Miller and Eric ([example post](https://homemlb.wordpress.com/2018/11/02/the-rest-of-our-negro-leagues-mles-centerfield-right-field-and-a-few-more-infielders/)), which I treated as a starting point consistently - they've gone and attempted to do fielding and league/park adjustments, yeoman's work. But I do go away from them because (1) they seem to be being overly swayed by sparse data, (2) they overrate the early and wartime Negro Leagues' level of competition, (3) there is sometimes more data available on baseball-reference than seamheads.com (which they use exclusively), and (4) they put more stock in runs than strikeouts, which only gets more problematic with less and crazier data.

  

My basic rule of thumb is something like 50 points of OBP and 125 points of slugging down for hitters, and about one K/9 down for pitchers.  
  
38\. C Josh Gibson (1937, Homestead Grays)

And here we find our first non-MLB entry. Has some Hornsby to him where his defense is just OK, but he plays a premium position and is a monster hitter. Negro Leagues translation can be a tricky business, but Gibson is in the sweet spot where it's both close enough to segregation that we can see the league translations with some confidence, and he's peaking before the war, when everyone's playing and the stats are well-kept. I'd believe a little higher or lower for Gibson, but I feel pretty good about him being the best non-MLB player ever.

****Masaichi Kaneda****  
****League quality is the tricky part with Japan. Much more than with the Negro Leagues, the change in quality of Japanese baseball over time is really obvious, but nailing down exactly when and how much it improves is the task at hand. I haven't really seen anyone take a good stab at historical translations, so you mostly just see Japan called "between AAA and major league" anywhere you look, which is true since about 1990 or so but obviously crazy going back.**

  

**Japan has good stats going back into the 30s, which is way before they approach major league level. Up through WWII, American teams would show up, goof around, enjoy the nightlife, and beat the snot out of the Japanese teams they faced every single game. There's a list in the back of Robert Whiting's fabulous** ***You Gotta Have Wa*** **of American-Japanese series results, and for a long time it's the U.S. teams winning 17-0 and 23-0-1, etc. I wouldn't normally put much stock into the outcomes of short exhibition series, but this is a level of dominance that you can't luck into, much closer to major league teams versus high school teams than minor league teams. For the period where there's no crossover between the leagues, it's clear that the Japanese teams are very far away, probably around the D-level minors at best before the 50s.**

  

**In 1950, we get our first crossover case, Wally Yonamine, born in Hawaii to Japanese parents, who goes from a good season at C-ball Salt Lake City to being one of the better players in NPB. The trickle begins, and by about 1960 it seems like most hitters who come over from AA are having a harder time in Japan than America. (It's worth noting here that, particularly in the early days, Japan is extraordinarily pitcher-friendly. Check out** [**Jiro Noguchi,**](https://www.baseball-reference.com/register/player.fcgi?id=noguch000jir) **who throws up consecutive seasons of a 0.93 and 0.88 ERA.) I've called it 75% of the majors. And this is an important era to get right, because it's not hard to convince yourself that Shigeo Nagashima and Katsuya Nomura would be top-tier MLB players from 1961-65, depending on how you read the league quality. Around 1964, it's about even with AAA (80% of the majors), and by 1972 or so, AAA guys coming over are clearly having a harder time hitting in Japan (85%). It's not until the early-mid 70s that MLB players are seeing Japan as a legitimate option, as opposed to a Hail Mary to keep their careers alive - we start seeing a couple players a year trickle over, usually after bad years, rather than heading to the minors.**

  

**One thing that makes the Japanese translations different from other leagues is that it seems pretty clearly true that Japanese umpires made the Americans earn it. It was important to Japanese baseball pride that their best players were better than the Americans who came over, and the umpires definitely had a Japanese strike zone and an American strike zone. Normally, this would just wash out in the translating process, but, before Hideo Nomo in 1995, the only Japanese player to play in the majors was Masanori Murakami, who pitched for San Francisco in 1964 & 1965.**

  

**There are also some legitimate park factor questions. It's definitely true that most of the early Japanese parks were small, so it's hard not to cast suspicion on the fact that Sadaharu Oh is hitting his homers in a park that's 288 feet down the lines. The odd part is that Oh hit basically the same number of homers at home and on the road, and that the** [**park factors**](http://npbstats.com/glossary/park-factors/) **for Korakuen Stadium during his career are only mildly hitter-friendly. It doesn't stand out that one park is small if they all are, essentially. And the parks that have survived until now are small-ish at worst. (Also, unlike old American parks, old Japanese parks are overwhelmingly symmetrical.) It's not the major issue I feared.**

**Craig Kimbrel (ATL, 2013). It's a similar story with Kimbrel. Nobody can compete with Rivera in longevity, but the strikeout numbers he posted in 2011 & 2012 are so impossible that his peak creeps out in front - in 139 innings, Kimbrel struck out 243 and walked 46. He strung together six or seven really dominant years.**

**Andrew Miller (CLE, 2016). The guy had a three-year stretch from 2014-16 where he struck out 326 and walked 46 over 198 innings. That's basically what Pedro did in 1999 & 2000. It's hard to beat Miller's peak for pure unhittability, although it does feel strange to top this list with a non-closer. The pro-Rivera argument revolves around the fact that he wasn't that much worse, but did it for far longer. Miller only noses ahead for about a two-year span, but he was better. As far as postseason performance goes, I think Rivera's extra credit there is mostly due to the Yankees being so good, and Miller's career postseason ERA is 0.95. Rivera was outstanding, but he still never had a postseason where he had 30 strikeouts and 12 hits allowed like 2016 Miller.**

**IN / OUT: SECOND BASEMEN**

**ALL-TIMERS MISSING: Ross Barnes would be the first player elected who played the majority of his career pre-National League - Al Spalding, Candy Cummings, and George Wright were all inducted as pioneers/executives.**

  

**SAFELY IN:**

**\#7 Charlie Gehringer (1937, DET): Another high-level defensive second baseman with an extended run of impressive hitting, though his 1934-37 triple-slash of .352/.437/.524 is even more superficially impressive than Sandberg's.**

**\#8 Bobby Grich (1975, BAL): This one always raises eyebrows. Deserving Gold Glove defender at a premium position, and a well above average hitter - obfuscated by playing his best years in a pitchers' park in the early 1970s and deriving most of his value from doubles and walks. Brooks Robinson is an interesting comp. Robinson had a little more power, but Grich got on base a little more; Brooks was an A++ fielder at third and at his best Grich was an A fielder at second. It's basically a wash.**

**\#9 Snuffy Stirnweiss**

**\#10 Joe Gordon (1942, NYY): Really good defensive second baseman who hits 30 homers a couple times and hit .322 / .409 / .491 in 1942. The ding on him is that his best season happens in 1942, when the league is starting to lose players to World War II. The counter is that Gordon's 1947 and 1948 seasons with Cleveland are in the same territory, and that his true peak may have been in his age-29 and -30 seasons in 1944 and 1945, when he was in the military. Hard to get him too far from the Hall of Fame line in either direction.**

**\#11 Robinson Cano (2013, NYY): For five years, from 2010-14, Cano was one of the best players in baseball. Well above average hitter and fielder.****  
****\#12 Chase Utley**

**\#13 Frankie Frisch (1927, STL): Not quite the hitter of Sandberg and Gehringer, but put up such outstanding defensive numbers in his 20s that he rates here anyway. Led the majors in steals twice.**

  

**BORDERLINE IN:**

**\#14 Craig Biggio****  
****\#15 Ryne Sandberg (1992, CHC): Was excellent in his MVP year of 1984, but more importantly hit .298/.365 /.513 from 1989-92 while winning three Gold Gloves.****  
****\#16 Tetsuto Yamada**

****Johnny Evers**

**Fred Dunlap (1884, SLM): Dunlap is tricky. He's one of the better players in baseball for the Cleveland Blues in the early 1880s, and I think he's got enough going to sneak across my Hall of Fame line by 1883. But in 1884, he plays for the St. Louis Maroons of the Union Association - which is officially recognized as Major League Baseball but is quite a bit worse than the 1884 National League - and hits an outrageous .412 / .448 / .621 as a legitimately very good defensive second baseman. He's the best player in the UA by ten miles, but then comes back to the NL the next year, after the UA folds, and hits .270 / .334 / .333 (although that makes the gap appear even bigger than it is). He was so good in 1884 that it probably was his actual peak, but it's tough to feel confident about it when his production curve looks so smooth otherwise. If you buy the UA improvement, which is certainly a bigger boost than most guys saw who jumped from the NL, he probably ought to be up there with Sandberg and Gehringer. If you ignore it entirely, his case is barely better than Cupid Childs's.**

**Frank Grant (1889, Cuban Giants): Fairly consistently regarded as the best black player of the 19th century. Black baseball stats before 1900 are essentially nonexistent, but Grant did play in the white minor leagues, with fairly complete stats, in 1886 and 1888, and we can see him be basically the best player in the International Association at age 20. (Though it's not like his stiffest competition turned out to be Honus Wagner, most of them do at least play in the major leagues.) His best seasons were probably in his mid-to-late-20s, like most players, and so this ranking is me betting on his improvement and his reputation. He probably ought to be in the MYSTERY category, but 1890 baseball is messy anyway and his minor league dominance at a young age made me comfortable enough to put him here.**

**Jose Altuve (2017, HOU): Definitely reached the point of best non-Trout player in baseball. One of the better hitters in the majors in 2016 & 2017 while playing a perfectly fine second base. This could wind up a controversially high ranking if Altuve falls apart in his 30s, but if he stays on his current track he'll be a Hall of Famer.**
