**COMPLETED: SP \#71: Bobby Mathews**

**The first pitch of the first game of the league that eventually developed into Major League Baseball was thrown by 19-year-old Bobby Mathews of the Kekionga club of Fort Wayne, Indiana, to Deacon White of the Forest City club of Cleveland, on May 4, 1871. Mathews was there as organized baseball began in earnest, and he saw the game take shape over the next 20 years, from the primordial ooze into something almost recognizable today.**

  

**There’s not a lot of personal information out there about the underrated 19th-century players. That said, it’s clear that Bobby Mathews fit in pretty well with early baseball, by which I mean he was pretty rough-and-tumble. He had some real issues with drinking. There’s reason to believe he got mixed up with gamblers from time to time. He never married, and some sources suggest he died of complications from syphilis.**

  

**Mathews was one of the first people to make “baseball player” into a career. He seems to have been a real student of pitching, one of the first. Lots of solid baseball players in his time period retired, essentially just to go be real adults. Bobby never seemed to seriously consider that path.**

  

**PHYSICAL DESCRIPTION: Stood 5’5” and weighed 140, which makes him an inch taller than Wee Willie Keeler, the smallest guy in this project. He started as a teenager, but even so, he outlasted every other early National Association pitchers by about a decade. At the beginning of his career, pitching meant throwing underhanded from 45 feet, without breaking your wrist, and lots of pitches were just ignored by umpires rather than being called balls or strikes. By the end, Bobby had survived the legalization of sidearm pitches and made it to the overhand era, they were down to five balls from a high of nine, the catchers were wearing equipment, and the pitching distance was up to 55’6”. He adapted quite a bit.**

**FASTBALL: Good.**

**OFF-SPEED: One of only a couple of pitchers who consistently threw a curveball in the early 1870s, and it was extremely effective. May have been the originator of the spitball, and, if not, certainly helped popularize it. Had a changeup. A true junkballer, maybe the original junkballer. Got a lot of strikeouts, for the time, largely on guile. CONTROL: In the first phase of his career, was below average at preventing walks, but walks were exceedingly rare league-wide. Turned into a very good control pitcher in the 1880s.**

**DURABILITY: Great. Threw basically every inning his teams played for the first six years of his career - in 1875, he threw 625 ⅔ of his team’s 636 innings. And in terms of longevity, like I said, he outlasted his whole generation of pitchers.** **  
****HITTING: Bad. We don’t think about it now, but early in pro baseball, pitchers were usually offering something with the bat. Bobby, on the other hand, was, if not the worst hitter in baseball during his career, certainly in the conversation.**

  

**Bobby Mathews was born in Baltimore in 1851 to a pair of Irish immigrants. In 1869, when Bobby was 17, he played for one of the first professional teams, the Marylands, in his hometown. The Marylands were successful enough to put together a barnstorming tour in 1870, but, after a pair of victories over Kekionga in Fort Wayne, had five players, including Mathews, poached by the losing team before they could leave town.**

  

**Kekionga made it into the newly formed National Association the following season, and Bobby pitched every inning of their 19 league games. He threw a shutout in the league’s opener, but Kekionga finished eighth of nine teams and folded in August. Bobby went home to Baltimore and started pitching for a new club there, nicknamed the Canaries, who found their way into the NA for the 1872 season and acquitted themselves well.**

  

**I’m not clear on who moved first, but in two separate signings, Mathews jumped to the New York Mutuals and was replaced in Baltimore by the Mutuals’ star pitcher, Candy Cummings. Bobby had led the league in strikeouts in 1872 with 57 in 406 innings, and led the league in strikeouts by huge margins his first two seasons in New York.**

  

**Certainly nobody today would say that strikeouts were high in 1877, but the leaguewide numbers from 1871-76 are much, much lower than any other year on record. This seems to be because umpires just chose to call neither strikes nor balls on many pitches - and, by rule, a player got one warning pitch before he was called out on strikes. Bobby Mathews was the game’s first strikeout artist; in both ‘73 and ‘74, he had essentially as many strikeouts as the \#2 and \#3 pitchers combined. Bobby was pretty clearly the best pitcher in baseball for those two seasons, but there just wasn’t enough hitting on the Mutuals, and Boston was absolutely loaded, so New York never really threatened a league title.**

  

**That said, New York was as good as anyone else in the NA. They weren’t alone in this era, but the Mutuals were widely suspected of being in the pockets of gamblers, predating and including the NA years. Bobby’s name comes up in a fair number of stories claiming that a game, lost by the Mutuals, was thrown. (There was even one incident where, after pulling Mathews early in a game due to a groin injury, the team later felt the need to produce a doctor’s note verifying the injury.) On the one hand, his name does come up pretty frequently, and Bobby Mathews was no choir boy. On the other, in 1876 he turned over a telegram from gamblers to the league and helped them in a sting operation to catch more gamblers. My inclination is to say that he got involved with gamblers and repented, but both of those claims are guesses.**

  

**The Mutuals went bankrupt and failed to make their final road trip of the 1876 season, so Bobby jumped to Cincinnati for 1877. He pitched badly for half a season before they folded as well. He bounced to the Janesville, Wisconsin, club of the League Alliance, then to the Alliance’s Brooklyn outfit to start 1878, then to Worcester midseason. He was kicked off the Worcester team for drunkenness - which I suspect was no easy task - though he was eventually re-instated. By the end of 1878, he was back in Baltimore again.**

  

**Bobby hopped to a very strong Providence squad, back in the National League, for the 1879 season. He pitched quite effectively as the \#2 pitcher behind John Ward, which here means starting about a third of the games while Ward pitched the rest. Bobby also saw some time in the outfield, odd because he was always such an awful hitter. With those two throwing every inning, two excellent outfielders in Paul Hines and Jim O’Rourke, and George Wright managing, the Grays beat Boston out for the NL title.**

  

**Mathews spent the 1880 season in San Francisco, where the three-team Pacific League began and nosedived within the space of a couple months. By the time he rejoined Providence, the season was over. For 1881, Bobby started as the third man behind 21-year-old Ward and NL rookie Charley Radbourn (probably a little too early in the career to refer to Charley as “Old Hoss”). Providence finished the season in second, but they were below .500 in mid-July when they kicked Mathews and catcher Emil Gross off the team for drinking too much. (Radbourn’s drinking was also noted by the club, but talent won out there, it appears.) After being cut, Bobby signed on with Boston, where he mostly played outfield and hit .169.**

  

**The 1882 Boston Red Stockings were one of the better teams in baseball, and they had a great pitching staff. Bobby had his best season in years, posting the highest strikeout rate anyone had seen to that point, 4.8 per nine innings. He was the \#2 starter to Grasshopper Jim Whitney, who was every bit as good as Bobby on the mound and about triple the hitter. The lineup wasn’t quite what you’d hope, and Boston finished third, but Mathews had re-established himself as one of the better pitchers in baseball at age 30. In August, with the season still in full swing, Bill Sharsig, owner of the new American Association’s Philadelphia club, caught up to Bobby in New York and signed him under the table for $1000 for 1883.**

  

**The sidearm pitch was legalized by the time the season started, and Bobby used it. He was the best pitcher on a championship-winning Philadelphia squad and one of the best in baseball. (It should be noted that the AA in 1883, though recognized as a major league by MLB today, was quite a bit worse than the National League.) By now, most teams, Philadelphia included, had some semblance of a rotation - Bobby threw a little under half the team’s innings on the season, and five different pitchers got real opportunities.**

  

**Bobby was a very good pitcher, though in a subpar major league, for Philadelphia for the next two seasons, but then his career started winding down. Before the 1886 season, he started working with the pitchers at the University of Pennsylvania. He struggled with arm injuries that year, and he wasn’t very effective when he did pitch. The club docked his pay $500, about 20% of his salary.**

  

**The next year, he coached at Penn again and struggled to crack a young rotation with Philly. He wound up making seven ugly starts and, at 35, finished his career as the all-time NA/NL/AA innings leader, not that anyone knew that at the time. He stayed on with Philly for 1888 as pitching coach, though he thought there was a chance he could get back into the mix as a player, as well. He was put in charge of the Philadelphia reserve squad and played a little for them, mostly in the field.**

  

**He spent a couple of years as an umpire in the Players’ League and the AA, having umpired about 40 games in the NA and NL as an active player (as many players had to in the 1870s). He never quite figured out how to transition out of life as an active player. An intriguing quote from an 1891** ***Sporting Life*****:** **“Bobby Matthews, the old pitcher, has joined in the tug-of-war craze and is forming an American team for the Philadelphia tournament.” He spent a lot of time at the racetrack. By 1895, he was mostly out of money and working for his old Mutuals and Grays teammate, retired first baseman Joe Start, who owned a roadhouse in Rhode Island.**

  

**In 1897, it became clear his health was declining. Bobby’s grip on reality loosened, and he claimed his nurses were trying to kill him. His memory got worse. His doctor sent him to the Spring Grove Hospital for the Insane, outside Baltimore. Bobby’s mother couldn’t pay his medical bills, and some charity games were set up to help her out. He spent the last six months of his life at his parents’ house in Baltimore, where he died in April 1898 at age 46.**

  
  

**1871 (FTW SP): \#5 pitcher in baseball****  
****1872 (BAL SP): \#5**

**1873 (NY Mutuals SP): \#1**

**1874 (NY Mutuals SP): \#1 (best season)**

**1875 (NY Mutuals SP): \#4**

**1876 (NY Mutuals SP): Top 10**

**1877 (CIN SP): Top 20**

**1878 (minors): Top 20**

**1879 (PRO SP): \#4 (champions)**

**1880 (minors): Top 20**

**1881 (PRO / BOS SP): \#20**

**1882 (BOS SP): \#3**

**1883 (PHA SP): \#5 (champions)**

**1884 (PHA SP): Top 20**

**1885 (PHA SP): Top 10**

**1886 (PHA SP):** second-best pitcher on the A’s  
**1887 (PHA SP):** mediocre starter, spent most of season in contract disputes

  
  

**COMPLETED: \#2 Babe Ruth**

  

Babe Ruth was an absolute raving lunatic, but everybody seemed to love him anyway.

  

It seems like he must have been either drinking, smoking, or cheating on his wives every moment he wasn't on the field. He loved gambling, both at the casinos and the horse track. He once was thrown out of a game for punching out an umpire, and he threatened to do so many more times. (It's truly astonishing how frequently, in biographer Robert Creamer's *Babe*, Ruth is quoted making the aggressive-but-oddly-endearing threat that he'll punch someone in the nose.) He could fart on command and kept a trophy in his house that he won in a farting contest. There's a story I just can't shake from my mind from *Babe*, where Ruth, at a party after a pennant-clinching win on the road, jumps up on a table with a beer in one hand and a sandwich in the other and yells, "Any girl who doesn't want to fuck can leave now\!" He once bedded every girl in a St. Louis whorehouse in succession.

  

But, for everyone who wasn't trying to keep him in line, Ruth's immeasurable charm, well-meaning nature, and guileless exuberance won out. Kids loved him, and he'd sign autographs all day long. It's shocking how many of his teammates spoke affectionately of him years later - you'd think he would've been a more divisive figure. (After eleven seasons together, he bade teammate Waite Hoyt farewell by saying, "Goodbye, Walter." Hoyt loved him.) His biggest issue was valuing having fun in the present over stability in the future, and he did it over and over and over again. When he inevitably did something stupid, he'd always own up to it and apologize, hat in hand. It didn't seem like he could hide anything from anybody, press included: He told the media when he wanted a raise, when he thought the manager was doing a bad job, when he was sad.

  

"Larger than life" seems like a descriptor created for Ruth. Witness after witness can't stop themselves from giving stunned descriptions of the sheer amount that he ate. At least three different times during his career, rumors went around through the media that Ruth had died, and Yankees owner Jacob Ruppert, it being the 20s and knowing who he was dealing with, went days at a time without knowing for sure. He was once knocked unconscious in an exhibition game after chasing a fly ball right into a palm tree. He signed for $80,000 in 1930 (about $1.2 million today), famously more than President Hoover, and twice as much as Rogers Hornsby, whose $40,000 a year deal was the second-highest in baseball. He grew up poor and wasn't afraid to spend the money he got - he was known for tipping more than the whole bill.

  

One night in 1920, he was drinking and driving with his wife and a couple of others, when he flipped the car in a one-car crash, nearly killing Yankees coach Charley O'Leary, who was with them. (Coach\!)

  

Once in 1921, after his teammates cooled him off, preventing a bar fight, he followed his opponent in his car. His teammates recognized that this was a problem and chased Ruth down, only to find him on the side of the highway being held by the man at gunpoint. (Pitcher Harry Harper drove his car directly at the man, and the Yankees successfully took his gun and got him to leave.)

  

One day in 1926, he had to spend a night in prison for reckless driving. He got his uniform delivered to the jail, was released into a throng of screaming fans, and then played in the game that night.

  

Ruth was genuinely hilarious, though it's hard to tell how often it was on purpose. President Coolidge came to watch the Yankees one day, and Ruth went over to shake his hand, introducing himself by saying, "Hot as hell, ain't it, Prez?" At a formal dinner party with Ford Frick (President of the National League for 20 years), he pushed away a salad without eating it, and was asked why by the hostess. It wasn't that the food didn't look good, "it's just that asparagus makes my urine smell," he told her kindly.

  

Even so, he won over many of his most aristocratic acquaintances with his joyfulness and sincerity. Especially early on, he was an easy target, as well - there's a famous story in which a teammate notices the young Ruth, after showering, put on the same sweaty underwear he'd worn during the game, which brought forth such severe razzing that Ruth supposedly never wore underwear again.

  

Management basically spent their time with Ruth begging him to chill out, knowing he wouldn't listen, then crossing their fingers and hoping he wouldn't die. It must have been exhausting, bordering on infuriating, for coaches and owners to watch a hungover Ruth be the best player in baseball - it's hard to convince the rest of your players that they need to take care of themselves with a walking counterexample in the next locker over. And it certainly didn't help that Ruth tried to fight nearly every manager he ever had, at one point or another.

  

It's an interesting thought experiment to consider how baseball history would've changed if Ruth had come up with, say, the Boston Braves, who were bad for pretty much his entire career. Would he have bothered to get back into shape once he got out of it? Would he have been written off as a drunken lout who didn't take the game seriously enough? Would home runs as a whole been considered empty calories for another decade or more?

  

Instead, he played for good teams, won a bunch of titles, and everyone who stuck with him tended to wind up rich and happy.

  

Here's some video of Ruth walking and talking, by way of a 1932 short film called "Just Pals". If you're only going to watch 10 seconds, I'd zoom in on the 7:40 range, where Ruth reacts to the kid finally getting a hit by manhandling the school headmaster:

  

\------

  

**PHYSICAL DESCRIPTION:** Ruth was a big dude, particularly for his time, listed at 6'2", 215. That's really his in-shape weight - he bounced around a decent amount, was over 250 at times. Check out how he's swimming in his Red Sox jersey here on the top left, is big but still in shape in the top right picture (1920), definitely expanding in the bottom left (1927), and completely out of shape with the Braves on the bottom right (1935):

  

This presentation makes it look more linear than it really was - there was a lot of showing up at camp out of shape and working his way back, and just general fluctuation - but it's worth remembering that, even though most of the images we see of Ruth are from that 1927 range and a bit after, during his best years he looked more like that 1920 picture. He used a huge bat, even for his time, almost always above 40 ounces and at times reaching into the high 40s.

  

**HIT:** Outstanding, not elite. He consistently placed among the league leaders in batting average, leading the AL once. It sounds harsh to ding him for never hitting .400, but in the 1920s there was a guy or two doing it every season. Ty Cobb hit .401 in 1922 at age 35, and for the 1921-25 seasons, combined, Rogers Hornsby hit .402. Ruth could hit, but he wasn't either of those guys in terms of hitting for average.

  

**POWER:** Almost certainly the greatest ever in comparison to his league. It's been repeated to death, but it really is unbelievable that he hit more homers than any other American League team in 1920. He hit 54 that year, and George Sisler finished second with 19 - it's kind of like if, say, Shohei Ohtani showed up last year and hit 135 homers. It's basically impossible to overstate the degree to which his power was unprecedented; I really do think we've been immunized to it because of the way the game followed him afterward. Even after strategy started catching up to him, he led the league in homers in 1931, which, if you'll recall, is halfway between those last two pictures above. Gehrig's the second-best power hitter around at that time, and he's 28 and in his prime, and Ruth's 36 and not taking care of himself in the slightest, and they tie, hitting in the same ballpark from the same side of the plate. It's just impossible. And in his youth, he was a pretty serious doubles hitter, as well.

  

**PLATE DISCIPLINE:** Elite. He essentially led the league in walk rate every year, with the exceptions of 1925 (when he wasn't right) and 1929 (when it plummeted, with no explanation I've discovered, only to rebound the following year). At the point of his retirement, he had six of the seven biggest walk seasons in baseball history. He certainly struck out his fair share as well, normally landing among the leaders, but at his peak a typical season was 150 walks, 85 strikeouts.

  

**RUN:** He was never fast, but it wasn't a disaster for most of his 20s. Obviously, by the time he's on the Braves, he's a slug, and he never really should've been trying to steal bases (though, being Ruth, he certainly wasn't about to let that stop him). But like I said above, legging out a double was definitely in play before 1925 or so, when he was 30.

  

**FIELD:** Same thing. Never good, but in his 20s he was fine, in his 30s he tipped into bad. Played a little more right field than left, but had a couple of seasons where he was mostly a left fielder, particularly 1919 and 1921.

  

**THROW:** Good arm.

  

\-----

  

And here's some shockingly high-quality video of him taking batting practice in 1931, looking pretty unimpressive:

  

He's worth discussing as a pitcher, as well.

  

**FASTBALL:** Definitely one of the harder-throwing pitchers in baseball, though never really considered to be the fastest.

**OFF-SPEED:** Threw a curveball, but it doesn’t seem to have been anything particularly special.

**CONTROL:** He was a pretty good strikeout pitcher for his time, but walked too many guys, as well. Control was average to below average.

  

**DURABILITY:** High, even for his time. Threw 300 innings more than once as a pitcher, really only missed time due to injury, at any time, with his mysterious 1925 illness.

  

\-----

  

​​George Ruth had caused enough problems by age seven that his father, a Baltimore bar owner who would not have been easily shocked, sent him to live at a reform boarding school in town for the rest of his youth. At 18, Ruth was pitching well enough for St. Mary’s School that the minor league Baltimore Orioles signed him, and at age 19, in July of 1914, the Red Sox purchased Ruth’s contract.

  

Ruth got off the train at Back Bay Station in Boston, and went to get breakfast at Landers Coffee Shop. His waitress was the then 16-year-old Helen Woodford, and they were married by October. This was moving even faster than it sounds, since Ruth was only in Boston for about one of the three months in between.

  

Ruth pitched twice for the Sox in July, then rode the bench for a month. The pitching went alright, so it seems like the benching must’ve been behavioral. Certainly, that refers to Ruth's constant partying, but more than that, it's the fact that the team's two biggest stars, Tris Speaker and Smoky Joe Wood, thought he was a screw-around. The Red Sox were a good team, led to the 1912 championship by Speaker and Wood, and second in the AL pennant race behind Philadelphia in mid-August. They had two young pitchers who liked to hit the town together, and one of them, Dutch Leonard, had an ERA below 1. Being a bad influence on a star player, without contributing, is a great way for a young player to get sent to the minor leagues, and Ruth was, on August 11, to the Providence Grays. He dominated for Providence, and, once they were eliminated from the pennant race, the Sox brought him back up for a couple of low-pressure development starts. In the offseason, he ran off with Helen to get married in Maryland (her parents weren't thrilled, and whose would be?), then worked behind his father's bar all winter.

  

He was a good, solid pitcher for the 1915 Red Sox and led the team with four home runs, which at that time was enough to rank in the top ten in the American League. It was immediately clear that something was unusual about Ruth as a hitter, and he was sent up to pinch-hit ten times. Boston rode the best pitching staff in the league to the 1915 World Series, which they won in five games over the Philadelphia Phillies. (Ruth's only appearance was a last-ditch pinch-hit groundout in Game 1.) It's been said that the reason he didn't pitch was because Philadelphia had so many good right-handed hitters, but, really, the Sox only used three pitchers the whole five games and it isn't clear Ruth was better than any of them.

  

Despite the title, there was quite a bit of offseason turnover in Boston. Wood barely pitched in the second half of the season due to a broken thumb, and would miss the entire 1916 campaign. And Speaker, after a 1915 season where he was the third-best player in baseball at age 27, was told by Red Sox owner Joe Lannin that his salary would be cut from $14,000 to $9,000 (from about $325,000 today to $200,000). Speaker understandably held out, Lannin sold him to Cleveland for $55,000 ($1.25 million today), and Speaker made $15,000. This made it Ruth and Leonard's team, and they were, short of Walter Johnson and Pete Alexander, the best pitchers in baseball in 1916.

  

​The Sox won their third World Series in five years that fall, over the Brooklyn Robins. Ruth only entered one game, starting Game 2, but it was a star-making turn. After allowing a first-inning homer to outfielder Hi Myers, Ruth threw thirteen shutout innings until Boston pinch-hitter Del Gainer finally singled across the winning run in the bottom of the 14th. Ruth likely would've pitched Game 6, but the Red Sox took the series in five games.

  

The most interesting game of Ruth's 1917 season was in June, though Boston finished second in the AL and Ruth was again one of the better players in baseball. In a start against Washington, Babe walked the leadoff hitter on four pitches and responded by punching the umpire, earning himself a ten-game suspension. (How could anyone be that riled up a minute into a game?) Ernie Shore relieved the ejected Ruth, the runner was thrown out stealing, and Shore retired the next 26 hitters for a unique combined no-hitter.

  

By the time the 1918 season began, there was discussion of using Ruth as a position player on the days that he wasn't pitching. In just under 400 plate appearances over the 1915-17 seasons, Ruth hit .302/.359/.479, which gave him the second-highest slugging percentage in baseball, behind Ty Cobb. The only hesitation was that he was one of the better pitchers around, and, even if he could keep up the hitting when playing every day, that his pitching might suffer too much for the experiment to be worthwhile. But Babe hit well the first month of the season, the rest of the Sox didn’t, and he found himself playing some first base in early May. By June he was playing nearly every day, whether at first or in center or left.

  

Ruth claimed that he either wanted to hit or pitch but not both, while manager Ed Barrow, for obvious reasons, wanted him to at least try. Seemingly, Ruth had some arm soreness that limited his pitching appearances in the summer, and, realizing this got him what he wanted, he continued to claim soreness long after it was really there. But he wound up making most of his turns in August, and pitched pretty well.

  

It's really the only year he was something like 50% pitcher and 50% hitter, and nobody in the majors has really had one like it since. He ranked in the top ten in the American League in ERA and tied for first in homers, with Philadelphia's Tillie Walker at 11 apiece. He wasn't as good as Cobb yet, but he was a legitimate sensation by this time, well-known for monster home runs, as hard as that is to imagine from a player with 11 on the season.

  

And then he came through with a pair of excellent pitching performances in yet another Red Sox World Series title, this time over the Cubs. Ruth tossed a complete game shutout in Game 1, then allowed two runs over eight innings and hit a triple in Game 5. (He didn't play the field in the Series, however.) Ruth was the most exciting player in baseball, and he was still three floors away from his ceiling.

  

Over the offseason, Ruth threatened to quit the sport and become a boxer if his $5000 salary ($83,000 today) wasn't doubled. Sox owner Harry Frazee was certainly annoyed by Ruth's salary demands, as well as his ongoing partying, but eventually folded, and Ruth rewarded Frazee with a spectacular season.

  

Barrow got Ruth to pitch some early in the season, but he was having his worst year on the mound and his best at the plate, and by August or so he was essentially a full-time hitter, as he was for the remainder of his big-league career. Ruth was the best player in baseball, and his 29 home runs was the new major league record, nearly tripling the next highest total in the American League. The record he broke was Ned Williamson's 1884 total of 27 homers, and Williamson played in a park with little league fences, less than 200 feet down either foul line. People lost their minds.

  

One of those people was Frazee, who couldn't help but notice that all these flashy home runs from his star hadn't been able to get the Red Sox into the top half of the American League in 1919. As often happens, the behavior that was considered merely "unprofessional" in a winning environment became "the cause of everything wrong with the organization" when the losses came in faster than expected. Then Ruth held out for double his salary, again. Frazee sold the 25-year-old Ruth to the New York Yankees for $100,000 ($1.45M today) and a $350,000 loan, and honestly, it seems like he felt great about it at the time. It certainly didn't hurt that Frazee wanted to put up theater productions on Broadway, and needed money to do so, but a big part of the decision was just bad talent evaluation.

  

It's hard to remember that the Yankees, at the beginning of the 1920 season, were one of the least successful franchises in the major leagues. They'd never made a World Series, and the best players in their 17-year history were probably a past-his-prime Home Run Baker and flash-in-the-pan pitcher Russ Ford. At the same time, they weren't a disaster when Babe actually showed up, returning all their key players from a team that finished in third place the previous season. (Strangely enough, the "Murderers' Row" moniker had already been used for the Yankee lineup before Ruth's arrival, despite the undeniable fact that it was mediocre at best.) The John McGraw / Christy Mathewson New York Giants were the legendary team in America’s biggest city, having won six pennants and a World Series since 1900. (And, it should be mentioned, they would go on to win the 1921 & 1922 World Series as well.)

  

Ruth exploded out of the gate for the Yankees and hit his unprecedented 30th home run of the season on July 19. He finished the season with 54 and set major league records for slugging percentage and walks, as well. It was the best season any baseball player had ever had, and it stayed that way for one year, until Ruth topped it in 1921. He broke his own record for home runs again, with 59.

  

The Yankees set a franchise record for wins in 1920, finishing a couple games behind pennant-winning Cleveland, then won 98 games on the way to the 1921 World Series against the crosstown Giants. Ruth was injured sliding in Game 3 - he fought through it for Games 4 & 5, but only had a pinch-hitting appearance afterward. He hit well when he played, but nobody else in the Yankee lineup did, and the Giants took the best-of-nine series in eight games.

  

After healing, Ruth set off on a postseason barnstorming tour to make some extra money - a fairly common practice at the time, but one which new Commissioner Kenesaw Mountain Landis was trying to curtail. Ruth called Landis and asked if he could go on the tour, Landis said absolutely not, so Ruth said he didn't care and started the tour anyway. Babe dodged Landis for awhile, had no interest in apologizing, and infuriated the commissioner in the process, before eventually shutting the exhibitions down earlier than planned. He wound up being fined his World Series' share and suspended six weeks. Ruth made up for his lost income by negotiating a new contract for $52,000 a year ($750,000 today), more than double Ty Cobb's league-leading $25,000 deal the previous season.

  

In his fifth game of the year, Ruth was called out sliding into a base, kicked dirt at the umpire in anger, and was thrown out of the game. As he walked off the field, he was heckled, and Ruth took off into the stands to fight the heckler, who escaped before Ruth got to him. In a postgame quote that would not have gone over well 100 years later, Ruth said, "I didn't mean to hit that umpire with the dirt, but I did mean to hit that bastard in the stands." American League president Ban Johnson announced that Ruth would be suspended, but Washington owner Clark Griffith had been promoting Ruth's arrival in Washington the following day and convinced Johnson to lift the suspension. Instead, Ruth was fined just $200 (about $3000 today). He was suspended again, though, later in the year, for threatening to fight an umpire. He also took a step back on the field, most likely because he never really got into playing shape during the first suspension - he really struggled in the first month after his return. He capped the rough season off by going 2-for-17 as the Yankees were swept by the Giants (with one tie) in the World Series.

  

Through the 1922 season, construction was happening on the brand new Yankee Stadium, which ownership was able to fund with the much-improved ticket sales since Ruth's addition. "The House That Ruth Built" opened for the following year. (There was a campaign to named it "Ruth Field" instead, which owner Jacob Ruppert eventually shot down.) Ruth got in shape and, in 1923, had probably the best season anyone’s ever had, before or since. He hit .393 and set the major league record for walks with 170, for an incredible .545 on-base percentage. In the third consecutive All-New York World Series, Ruth was dominant as the Yankees won their first title.

  

Ruth's first child, Dorothy, was born in 1921. Helen had had four miscarriages before Babe convinced her to adopt, although it's unclear whether Helen was made aware that Dorothy's biological parents were Ruth and his mistress, Juanita Jennings (a married New York hand model). Helen, and then Dorothy, continued to live much of the year in Sudbury, MA, even after Ruth's sale to New York, and the Babe had not taken naturally to long distance monogamy. In 1923, Ruth met Claire Hodgson, a 25-year-old New York model and theater actress who had been widowed a few years previously. The situation was much closer to a real relationship than most of Ruth's trysts, and, while his marriage to Helen was, understandably, well on its way to disintegrating, divorce wasn't considered an option because both he and Helen were Catholic.

  

Insomuch as having yet another all-time season on a second-place team could be considered pedestrian, 1924 was a pedestrian one for Ruth. The following year was not.

  

After showing up to Yankees spring training in Arkansas at a reported 260 pounds, Ruth collapsed on the train as the team started to head back north to start the season. (This is the most famous of the times Ruth was rumored to have died - multiple newspapers printed obituaries.) Upon arriving, he collapsed again, and was hospitalized for weeks. It remains a mystery just what happened here, though it seems the most likely explanation is an intestinal abscess brought on by excessive eating and drinking. Thirty pounds melted off him in the hospital after surgery, and management wasn't sure if he should try to come back at all that season. He didn't play his first game until June 1 and was a shell of himself for the rest of the year. He was still the best hitter on the Yankees, when he played, but was a far cry from how he'd hit in even his suspension year, or his last year with Boston. The Yankees finished seventh in the AL, the only time in Ruth's tenure that they ended the year outside the top three.

  

By this point, the Babe was 30, way out of shape, and coming off his worst season as a hitter. He'd never really taken care of himself, and there wasn't much reason to believe he'd start now. There was a sense that Ruth might be done as the best player in baseball, not least of all because Rogers Hornsby was coming off his second straight year hitting .400.

  

​Instead, Babe got a real trainer, worked his way back into shape, and was utterly dominant again in 1926. He had a formidable sidekick now in the young Lou Gehrig, who was quickly becoming one of the best players in the majors, and the Yankees won the American League pennant again. Ruth's comeback season was highlighted by baseball's first-ever three-homer game in the World Series, but the Yankees lost the Series to St. Louis in seven games.

  

Down to their final out in a winner-take-all Game 7, Ruth drew a bases-empty walk against Cardinals' ace Pete Alexander, who was pitching in relief. The Yankees trailed, 3-2, and Bob Meusel, one of their most dangerous hitters, stepped up to face Alexander. Inexplicably, on the first pitch, Ruth bolted for second base and was thrown out easily. Suddenly, the season was over, and the Cardinals were champions. Ruth shuffled off the field quietly. He said later that he thought he could steal the base because nobody would be expecting it, and then he'd be able to score on a single from Meusel. Regardless, after his ill-advised attempt, he'd played seven seasons in New York and come away with one championship.

  

The 1927 season is notable for two main reasons. First, Ruth hit his career-high 60 home runs, a record that stood until Roger Maris broke it in 1961. Second, that year saw the Yankees become one of the great teams in baseball history, as Gehrig exploded and the pitching improved. They won 110 games and swept Pittsburgh in the Series, getting Ruth his fifth ring. And they followed it up with another dominant season in 1928, with a sweep over St. Louis, making it six.

  

In January 1929, Helen died in a house fire, by which time when Ruth was all but living with Claire. While legitimately devastated by Helen's passing, he married Claire, and adopted her 13-year-old daughter, Julia, in April. Babe couldn't walk all over Claire the way he had Helen, and she quickly became nearly as important as he was in business decisions. (Legendary Philadelphia A's owner/manager Connie Mack said, after considering hiring Ruth as manager, that Claire would be running the team within a month if he did - and that wasn't an insult to Claire.)

  

Claire in Babe’s *Fancy Curves*: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=65ZHvHDnmfM

  

For the next three years, Ruth’s age 34-36 seasons, he continued to be the best player in baseball, but New York took a step back as the pitching fell apart, and Philadelphia took all three pennants. Ruth's salary peaked at $80,000 in 1930 & 1931 ($1.15M today), to much pearl-clutching from the public - no other player reached that figure until Joe DiMaggio in 1949. Yankee manager Miller Huggins passed away after the 1929 season, and Ruth campaigned to replace Huggins as a player-manager, citing the examples of Ty Cobb and Tris Speaker. (Legends who, for one thing, were no longer managing, but also guys who breathed baseball in a way Ruth just didn’t.) He would’ve been the only player-manager in baseball to start 1930, and he was never seriously considered for the job. From then until his retirement, Ruth continued to ask for the job, and the Yankees did everything they could to keep him happy without giving it to him. (And not without reason, I might add - Ruth would’ve been a terrible manager.) It’s tough to go from problem child to a management position, and that was no different in Ruth’s case.

  

His reign as baseball’s best player lasted from 1919-1932, with blips here and there where Hornsby nosed ahead of him. By 1932, young catcher Bill Dickey had come into his own, and the New York pitching staff was back to above average. The Yankees marched to the pennant and swept Chicago in a Series where the most interesting moment had little impact on the outcome.

  

In the fifth inning of Game 3, legend has it that Ruth pointed his bat toward centerfield, and on the next pitch hit a home run that landed right where he pointed. Mythmakers were excited to report that the Babe "called his shot," though whether he really did is disputed. I'd only note that Ruth must have boasted that he was about to hit a home run 100 times in his career, because that was the type of thing he liked to do, and, because he was an incredible power hitter, I'm sure he followed through a few times as well. Maybe that's what he did here, but I don't think he did so obviously enough to alert Cubs pitcher Charlie Root, because it sounds like Root was a mean cuss who would've thrown the next pitch at Ruth's head for far less. Anyway, here's the video:

  

Ruth was getting further and further out of shape, and more and more insistent on the managing job, but he continued to be one of the better players in baseball for the next two years, at age 38 and 39. He was no longer as good as Gehrig, and he had taken a clear step back from what he’d been, but he still finished second in the AL in homers in 1933 and eighth in 1934. But by this point, he was openly talking to the press about wanting the manager's job (which would involve firing Joe McCarthy, who had just led the Yankees to one title and eventually would lead them to six more), and ownership was looking for a way to get rid of him, preferably gracefully. So the Yankees were making it clear to other teams that Ruth was very much available if he wanted to go, and the Boston Braves realized that they could use Ruth's managerial ambitions to take advantage of the tickets he'd sell.

  

Braves owner Emil Fuchs talked to Ruth and implied that, by being assistant manager to Bill McKechnie in 1935, he'd be next in line, and that McKechnie was considering a move to the front office in the very near future. Fuchs also offered him a portion of the box office receipts and an opportunity to buy a piece of the organization, and Ruth was convinced. The Yankees were thrilled that they could peaceably detach themselves and let him walk.

  

Ruth made it through May and retired. He was clearly far out of baseball shape. That said, it's pretty shocking the way his numbers look, well, better than most of the rest of the Braves. He was only hitting .181, which would've been frowned upon in 1935, but despite playing just 28 of the worst games he'd ever played, he finished second on the team in homers and OBP. His fielding appears to have sunk past bad and into unplayable territory (it's said that some of the pitchers threatened to strike if he continued to play the field), but even at the grisly end he was getting on base and hitting for power.

  

After his playing career ended, Ruth did not age gracefully. His only post-retirement job in baseball was a stint as first-base coach for Brooklyn in 1938. He gained more weight, played golf, and showed up in exhibition games whenever he could. Ruth visibly missed the days when he was on top of the world. He did not easily let go of his glory days.

  

In 1947, he was hospitalized, and the doctors found a malignant tumor in his neck. Claire and Ruth's closest friends decided not to tell him about the cancer, though it eventually became obvious he was dying, as he started to lose weight rapidly and struggled to keep food down. The Yankees held a Babe Ruth Day near the end, where he received one final hero's welcome and thanked the fans for their support over the decades. He died the following year, at age 53.

  

\-----

  

**1914 (BOS SP):** most of year in minors

**1915 (BOS SP):** fourth-best pitcher on the Red Sox (WS)

**1916 (BOS SP): \#5 pitcher in baseball (WS)**

**1917 (BOS SP): Top 10**

**1918 (BOS LF): \#3 position player in baseball, fairly even split between pitching and hitting (WS)**

**1919 (BOS LF): \#1**

**1920 (NYY RF): \#1**

**1921 (NYY LF): \#1**

**1922 (NYY LF): \#2 (behind Hornsby)**

**1923 (NYY RF): \#1 (best season of all-time) (WS)**

**1924 (NYY RF): \#2 (behind Hornsby)**

**OUT: Bill Joyce**

**PHYSICAL DESCRIPTION: 5'11, 185 pounds, which was average to a little above average at the time. Left-handed hitter, crowded the plate. Used a bat painted blue around the end, visible in the picture above once you know it's there.**

**HIT: Solid. Was a .300 hitter, but in a time when a lot of guys were.**

**POWER: Very good. Led the league in homers in 1896 and finished second three other times in just eight seasons.**

**RUN: Good. Stole 40 bases twice and was good for 30 most years - though that was under the old definition, where taking an extra base counted as a steal, and 30-steal guys were fairly common. Double-digit triples every year.**

**DEFENSE: Average to below average. Good enough to stick at third, but nothing to get excited about. Threw the ball away a fair amount.**

**PLATE DISCIPLINE: Excellent. Drew 100 walks in a season twice, which was unusual in the 1890s. Typically toward the top of the league.**

**DURABILITY: Definite issues. Missed a fair amount of time in three different seasons with lower body injuries. Only played the eight years, but it was due to personality, rather than health, that he was out of the game so quickly.****  
****Born in St. Louis to Irish parents, played professionally in Texas leagues from age 18 in 1886 through 1889. In the fall of 1889, Charlie Comiskey and the St. Louis Browns were barnstorming through Texas and were impressed by Joyce. When John Montgomery Ward, architect of the new Players' League, was stuck without a third baseman for his team - the Ward's Wonders, based in Brooklyn - Comiskey pointed him in Joyce's direction.**

  

**After a solid rookie year and the quick demise of the Players' League, Joyce jumped to the American Association's Boston Reds, where he played great for a couple months until he broke his ankle sliding into second base. (The Reds cruised to the American Association championship, regardless.) For the second year running, Joyce's team and league disintegrated, and he wound up back in Brooklyn with Ward, playing for the National League's Grooms. He was good again, and again missed a decent chunk of the season with a leg injury.**

**​**

**After the 1892 season, Brooklyn, at least partially because of Joyce's attitude, sent him to Washington for $2000 ($56K today, a little less than Joyce was making at the time) and good-field, no-hit shortstop Danny Richardson. Joyce held out for the entire 1893 season before eventually reporting to Washington at the start of 1894, where he was dominant for a terrible Senators team. At .355 / .496 / .648, he was one of the better hitters in the league, though he missed some time at the end of the season after hip surgery.**
